| \nThe evaluation protocol.\nEvery optimizer is driven through the same interface, the same GFN2-xTB backend ([Bannwarth et al., 2019](#bib.bib9); [Bannwarth et al., 2020](#bib.bib10)), the same budget of force calls (200, and 500 on Solvated PubChem, as the benchmark specifies), and the same five Gaussian-style convergence criteria, on all four benchmark sets.\nEach optimizer’s own stopping test is disabled.\nOne optimizer required non-default settings forced by this system class rather than chosen: PreconLBFGS uses the exponential preconditioner with explicit \\(r_{\\text{NN}}=1.5\\) Å and \\(r_{\\text{cut}}=3.0\\) Å, because the automatic neighbour-cutoff estimate does not terminate for an isolated molecule in no cell; the two radii bracket a covalent bond and its second shell, the range the preconditioner is built for.\nTable [5](#A6.T5) reports force-call cost \\(C_{\\text{per-mol}}\\) and \\(\\bar{r}\\) for the reference and all 26 classical configurations on each of the four sets, in the units of Table [1](#S4.T1), and Table [6](#A6.T6) applies the structural criteria of Appendix [E](#A5) to every one of them.\nSella has the lowest per-molecule force-call cost among the tested classical optimizers on all four datasets, supporting its selection as the starting point for evolution.\nOur experiments show that the choice of a coordinate system plays a crucial role in the optimization process. On Dipeptides, pysisyphus in TRIC coordinates reaches the same minimum as the reference on \\(95.8\\%\\) of molecules and geomeTRIC in TRIC on \\(95.4\\%\\), while Cartesian BFGS manages \\(23.3\\%\\) and FIRE reaches it on none of the 673 — its median heavy-atom RMSD there is \\(0.56\\) Å, not a marginally different conformer but a different structure. This is the mechanism behind the cost column: Cartesian methods are not merely slower, they frequently stop somewhere else.\nF.2 What actually makes an optimizer efficient\nShajan et al. ([Shajan et al., 2023](#bib.bib17)) identify two factors behind the efficiency of a geometry optimizer: the coordinate system in which it takes its steps, and the initial guess for the Hessian.\nOn the Baker set ([Baker, 1993](#bib.bib18)) they find \\(20\\)–\\(24\\) steps per molecule in Cartesian coordinates with a unit initial Hessian, \\(11\\)–\\(12\\) in internal coordinates with a unit Hessian, and \\(6\\)–\\(10\\) in internal coordinates with a model Hessian.\nEach of these results, however, comes from a different program, so the two factors cannot be separated from each other or from differences in implementation, as the authors note.\nOur panel allows a direct test: we change one factor at a time inside a single program and keep everything else fixed.\nCoordinate system. In geomeTRIC ([Wang and Song, 2016](#bib.bib7)) we keep the step algorithm, trust radius and Hessian guess the same and change only the coordinates.\nRelative to Sella, the cost on Test rises from \\(129.6\\%\\) with TRIC and \\(130.4\\%\\) with delocalized internals ([Baker et al., 1996](#bib.bib20)), through \\(145.9\\%\\) with primitive internals and \\(327.2\\%\\) with HDLC ([Billeter et al., 2000](#bib.bib21)), to \\(572.2\\%\\) with Cartesian coordinates: the coordinate system alone changes the cost by a factor of \\(4.4\\).\nInitial Hessian. In pysisyphus we keep redundant internal coordinates and change only the initial Hessian. The cost is \\(110.5\\%\\) with the Fischer–Almlöf model ([Fischer and Almlof, 1992](#bib.bib25)), \\(110.8\\%\\) with Lindh’s ([Lindh et al., 1995](#bib.bib24)), \\(130.5\\%\\) with Swart’s ([Swart and Matthias Bickelhaupt, 2006](#bib.bib26)), \\(193.2\\%\\) with a simple diagonal guess and \\(444.9\\%\\) with the unit matrix: the initial Hessian alone changes the cost by a factor of \\(4.0\\).\nBoth factors therefore matter, and by a similar amount. These factors are averaged over the molecules that both the method and Sella converged, so they are only lower bounds: the worst settings also fail on more molecules — geomeTRIC with Cartesian coordinates leaves 141 of the 500 Test molecules unconverged, and pysisyphus with the unit Hessian 56.\nThe consequence for the present work is that, on single-fragment molecules, Sella’s margin over a well-configured generic optimizer is small.\npysisyphus with delocalized internal coordinates needs \\(105.4\\%\\) of Sella’s force calls on Test and \\(103.6\\%\\) on Dipeptides; with redundant internals and the Fischer–Almlöf initial Hessian — the same model Hessian Sella uses ([Hermes et al., 2021](#bib.bib4)) — it needs \\(110.5\\%\\) and \\(106.0\\%\\). These averages are over molecules both optimizers converged; pysisyphus also fails on 8 (delocalized) and 34 (redundant) of the 500 Test molecules, all of which Sella converges.\nAlmost all of the \\(5\\)–\\(6.5\\times\\) advantage that Sella holds over Cartesian methods therefore comes from its choice of internal coordinates and a model initial Hessian, rather than from its step algorithm.\nF.3 The optimizers\nThe evolved optimizers, AutoSella-F and AutoSella-A, are described in Section [3.5](#S3.SS5) and Appendix [C.2](#A3.SS2).\nThis section describes the classical optimizers of the panel, all run under the same force-call budget and convergence test.\nSella\n([Hermes et al., 2022](#bib.bib3)) performs restricted-step partitioned rational-function optimization in redundant internal coordinates, with iterative partial Hessian diagonalization, TS-BFGS-style Hessian updates, automatic dummy-atom handling for linear bends, and geodesic stepping on the internal-coordinate manifold ([Hermes et al., 2021](#bib.bib4)).\nIt is the reference throughout this work.\nConfigured in Cartesian coordinates it costs \\(5\\)–\\(6\\times\\) more, which is the single largest configuration effect we measured.\ngeomeTRIC\n([Wang and Song, 2016](#bib.bib7)) introduces translation–rotation internal coordinates (TRIC), which add the translations and rotations of each molecular fragment to the primitive internal set.\nIt uses BFGS with a Schlegel-model initial Hessian ([Bernhard Schlegel, 1984](#bib.bib23)) and supports Cartesian, primitive, delocalized ([Baker et al., 1996](#bib.bib20)) and hybrid-delocalized ([Billeter et al., 2000](#bib.bib21)) coordinates.\nIt is second on step count in Shajan et al. ([Shajan et al., 2023](#bib.bib17)).\nHere it trails pysisyphus on single-fragment molecules, but its TRIC coordinates make it the most reliable classical optimizer on the multi-fragment sets: it converges on 99 of the 100 solvated systems.\npysisyphus\n([Steinmetzer et al., 2020](#bib.bib8)) is a Python toolkit for exploring potential energy surfaces: rational-function optimization ([Banerjee et al., 1985](#bib.bib22)), chain-of-states methods, and IRC.\nIt exposes the coordinate system and the model initial Hessian as independent options — Fischer–Almlöf ([Fischer and Almlof, 1992](#bib.bib25)), Lindh ([Lindh et al., 1995](#bib.bib24)), Swart ([Swart and Matthias Bickelhaupt, 2006](#bib.bib26)), simple, or unit — and is consequently the only package in the panel that can separate the two factors.\nWith delocalized internal coordinates it is the strongest classical optimizer on single-fragment molecules, at \\(105\\%\\) of Sella’s force calls on Test and \\(104\\%\\) on Dipeptides; its variants that bridge fragments with artificial bonds, however, fail on most multi-fragment systems.\nPyBerny\n([Hermann, 2021](#bib.bib2)) is an open implementation of the Berny algorithm used in Gaussian: quasi-Newton steps in redundant internal coordinates with an iterative Hessian estimate, a trust region, line search and coordinate weighting, accelerated by GDIIS ([Császár and Pulay, 1984](#bib.bib27)) and its energy-represented variant GEDIIS ([Li and Frisch, 2006](#bib.bib28)).\nIt ties with Sella for fewest steps in Shajan et al. ([Shajan et al., 2023](#bib.bib17)), but under our tighter five-criterion test it costs \\(2.0\\times\\) on Test and \\(2.5\\times\\) on Dipeptides, and reaches the reference minimum on only \\(62.7\\%\\) of Dipeptides.\nOn the solvated systems it cannot build its internal coordinates for 63 of 100 molecules.\nASE BFGS, LBFGS and their line-search variants\n([Hjorth Larsen et al., 2017](#bib.bib6)) are\nCartesian quasi-Newton methods; LBFGS uses the limited-memory update ([Nocedal, 1980](#bib.bib30); [Liu and Nocedal, 1989](#bib.bib29)).\nTheir initial inverse Hessian is a scaled identity (\\(\\alpha=70\\) by default).\nThey cost \\(5.5\\)–\\(6.7\\times\\) the reference.\nBFGSLineSearch is reported elsewhere as the most reliable optimizer for landing in the correct basin; here it is the most reliable of the Cartesian quasi-Newton group on energy fidelity while also the most expensive of them.\nFIRE\n([Bitzek et al., 2006](#bib.bib11)) and FIRE2 ([Guénolé et al., 2020](#bib.bib12)) are damped molecular-dynamics minimizers with adaptive time-stepping, widely used for their robustness and matrix-free cost per step.\nOn drug-like molecules under a 200-call budget they under-relax: median energy recovery falls to \\(0.92\\)–\\(0.93\\) of the reference on Dipeptides, and neither reaches the reference minimum on any dipeptide.\nSciPy CG and BFGS\n([Virtanen et al., 2020](#bib.bib16)) are the Polak–Ribière conjugate-gradient and BFGS implementations wrapped by ASE.\nSciPy BFGS tracks ASE BFGS closely, as expected; conjugate gradient is consistently worse on energy fidelity.\nODE12r\n([Makri et al., 2019](#bib.bib13)) is an adaptive-timestep ODE solver originally developed for minimum-energy-path finding, applied here as a minimizer. Like FIRE it under-relaxes on flexible molecules.\nPreconLBFGS\n([Packwood et al., 2016](#bib.bib14)) applies a preconditioner to L-BFGS; the Exp preconditioner used here is built from interatomic distances.\nIt is the best-performing Cartesian-space method in the panel at \\(4.4\\)–\\(4.6\\times\\), consistent with the original report that preconditioning helps most for large systems — our molecules, at 3–110 atoms, are below the regime where the published order-of-magnitude gains appear.\nAppendix G Optimizer components and ablation reproducibility\nThis section interprets the improvements proposed by autoresearch (AR) by grouping the evolved mechanisms into functional blocks and measuring the effect of removing them. We compare against the Sella with allow_fragments=false used at the beginning of evolution. The blocks summarize the implemented improvements related to a distinct mechanism in the optimizer (e.g. Hessian initialization or step size control).\nG.1 Experimental design\nEach single-block ablation starts from AutoSella-A/F and disables one or two family of changes. It therefore measures that family’s effect conditional on the remaining evolved mechanisms. All variants are evaluated on both training and validation splits. The compared metric is \\(C_{\\text{per-mol}}\\) from Eq. [1](#S3.E1), the arithmetic mean of per-molecule force-call ratios. A valid evaluation requires complete coverage, no optimizer errors, and mean normalized energy recovery \\(\\bar{r}\\geq 1-10^{-9}\\) (Eq. [2](#S3.E2)).\nG.2 AutoSella-F blocks\nA: Geometry initialization.\nBefore the first call to the potential, AutoSella-F adjusts the input geometry in three ways. None of them calls the potential, so they cost nothing in the force-call budget.\nGeometric rules. Some groups in the input start in a chemically known unfavourable conformation and are moved to a favourable one by a fixed geometry-based rule that depends only on bonding pattern. A methyl group whose C–H bonds start lined up with the bonds of the neighbouring atom, which is the top of its rotation barrier, is rotated so that they lie in between those bonds instead; next to a C=O group the preferred orientation is the opposite, with one C–H lined up with the C=O bond. An atom that is normally pyramidal, such as three-coordinate phosphorus, is bent out of plane when it starts flat. Most rules reflect standard conformational preferences.\nFragment pre-optimization.\nIn a system of several molecules, the molecules are first moved and rotated as rigid bodies to minimize a simple model of their electrostatic and van der Waals interaction.\nPartial charges come from formal charges and bond polarities, and the van der Waals terms use UFF parameters ([Rappé et al., 1992](#bib.bib63)).\nThis brings the molecules close to a favourable mutual arrangement before the first call to the potential.\nSymmetry breaking. Finally, in a system of several fragments, each fragment is shifted as a whole by 0.02 Å in a fixed pseudo-random direction, so that the optimizer does not stall at a point that is stationary only because of an exact symmetry of the start.\nThe ablation removes all three.\nB: Fragment handling.\nWhen a system consists of several disconnected fragments, Sella can describe each fragment by its own position and orientation in addition to its internal coordinates, so that fragments move relative to one another as rigid units. Without this option, Sella instead links the fragments by artificial bonds that exist only in its coordinate system, not in the potential. The starting program of the evolution did not use the option, and AutoSella-F enabled it early on; the reference Sella in this work uses it as well, so this block is shared with the reference rather than an advantage over it. The ablation switches the option off.\nC: Model Hessian and coordinates.\nSella starts from an approximate Hessian built from the structure alone, in which every bond, angle and dihedral is assigned a stiffness by simple rules. AutoSella-F changes this model in three ways.\nStiffness by chemical type. The stiffnesses of the existing coordinates depend on more detailed chemical classes, for example the kind of single bond a rotation is about, nearly linear groups, and bonds to heavier elements and metal ions.\nNon-covalent contacts. New terms describe interactions the original model ignores: hydrogen bonds, contacts of X–H groups with aromatic rings, and atoms close in space but far apart along the chain of bonds. These are not new coordinates: their stiffness is computed for the atoms involved and projected onto the existing internal coordinates.\nCoordinates. The evolution extends Sella’s set of redundant internal coordinates with out-of-plane coordinates for planar groups such as carbonyls. They describe bending of such a group out of its plane directly, which the original set captured only indirectly through dihedral angles.\nThe ablation restores Sella’s original model and coordinates.\nD: Hessian updates.\nA quasi-Newton optimizer refines its Hessian from pairs of a step and the resulting change of the gradient, each of which measures the curvature along that step. AutoSella-F improves this update in three ways.\nMemory. Sella uses only the latest pair; AutoSella-F imposes the last four at once, discarding older pairs that are inconsistent with the newest one.\nFollowing the geometry. The part of the model Hessian that depends on interatomic distances, the stiffness of bonds and non-covalent contacts, is recomputed at every new geometry, and the stored pairs, measured at earlier geometries, are corrected by the same change so that they describe the curvature at the current point.\nSymmetry. If the molecule is symmetric, the curvature along a step equals the curvature along its symmetric image, for example its mirror image. Every stored pair is therefore also entered in its symmetric copies, which gives additional curvature information.\nThe ablation removes all three and returns to Sella’s update from the latest pair alone.\nE: Step control.\nSella limits each step to a trust radius, which it enlarges or shrinks after every step according to how well the model predicted the change in energy. AutoSella-F changes this control in three ways.\nTrust radius. The radius starts larger, grows faster after well-predicted steps and is halved after a failed one.\nAtomic displacement. For single molecules, a step is additionally limited so that no atom moves by more than twice the trust radius.\nStep refinement. For single molecules, the step is refined before it is taken: the stiffness of non-covalent contacts is re-evaluated along the proposed step, and the step is recomputed with it, at most twice and without calls to the potential.\nThe ablation restores Sella’s radius rules and removes the displacement limit and the refinement.\nG.3 AutoSella-A blocks\nA: Fragment handling.\nAs in block B of AutoSella-F (Section [G.2](#A7.SS2.SSS0.Px2)), AutoSella-A enables Sella’s fragment option; in addition, the initial stiffness of the fragment position and orientation coordinates is ten times smaller (\\(0.005\\) instead of \\(0.05\\) Hartree), so fragments move more freely at the start.\nThe ablation switches the option off and restores the original stiffness.\nB: Model Hessian and coordinates.\nThe approximate Hessian that Sella starts from is rebuilt from published stiffness rules: Badger’s rule for bonds, Schlegel’s for angles and torsions and Fischer’s for out-of-plane bending, together with extra springs between atoms of the same molecule. In conjugated hydrocarbons, the model also couples the stretching of neighbouring bonds, which share their \\(\\pi\\) electrons. The set of internal coordinates gains out-of-plane coordinates averaged over atom orderings, bending coordinates for nearly linear groups, and torsions across linear chains. The ablation removes all of these additions.\nC: Calibration.\nThe model Hessian is a sum of parts — bonds, angles, torsions, out-of-plane terms and a few smaller ones — each part scaled by a single factor that starts at one. After the first six usable steps, these factors are fitted to the curvature measured along those steps, within a factor of two of their initial values, and the Hessian updates are then reapplied. The ablation keeps all factors at their initial values.\nD: Coordinate transformation.\nBond lengths are replaced by a nonlinear function of the length, chosen after Badger’s rule so that the energy is closer to quadratic in it. When a step in internal coordinates is converted back to atomic positions, the conversion is updated along the way instead of being held fixed. When a new covalent contact forms, the set of internal coordinates is rebuilt. The ablation restores plain bond lengths, the fixed conversion and the original rebuilding rule.\nE: Hessian update.\nThe update mixes Sella’s usual formula with a rank-one correction, weighted by how well each fits the latest step. Directions in which the model has negative curvature are treated as positive, both when a step is computed and when the Hessian is updated. The ablation restores Sella’s original update.\nF: Collision and step control.\nThe initial trust radius is larger for single molecules (\\(0.2\\)) than for fragment positions and orientations (\\(0.1\\)). Before the potential is called, a step that would push non-bonded atoms into each other is shortened, and the trust radius is adjusted for the shortened step. The ablation restores the original radius and removes the collision check.\nG: Gaussian process.\nFrom the recent energies and gradients, AutoSella-A fits a Gaussian-process model of the energy in a small space spanned by the ordinary step and recent displacements. Minimizing this model proposes an alternative step, which is taken only if it passes checks on descent, size, predicted gain and uncertainty; otherwise the ordinary step is used. Fitting and minimizing the model needs no additional calls to the potential. The ablation always takes the ordinary step.\nG.4 Dependencies, results, and unsuccessful evaluations\nWe supplement all single block removals with joint removals: Fable C & E and Astra B & C, B & E, and B & G. These pairs probe the contact model and its step correction, and the physical prior’s connections to calibration, curvature treatment, and the surrogate. For all four tested pairs on validation, joint removal increases cost less than the sum of the individual removal penalties. Thus, the single-block effects cannot be treated as independent, additive contributions.\nFigure [3](#A7.F3) reports every measured variant. The full\nAutoSella-F costs are 65.13% and 64.99% of Sella on the training and\nvalidation splits; AutoSella-A costs are 74.51% and 76.66%.\nThe physical-model and coordinate family has the largest measured\nsingle-removal cost increase in both series: Fable C adds 15.57 and\n18.68 percentage points, while Astra B adds 8.82 points on validation;\nthe latter’s training result fails the energy criterion. Removing Fable D\nadds 9.66 and 10.16 points.\nOf all evaluations, only five complete evaluations fall below the energy threshold: Fable \\(-A\\) on training; Astra \\(-B\\), \\(-B\\) & \\(-G\\), and \\(-F\\) on training; and Astra \\(-A\\) on validation. Their measured costs remain visible as red crosses. And only three evaluations contain optimizer errors. This indicates that described blocks represent meaningful self-contained families of improvements with little or no effect on adjacent blocks.\nAppendix H Autoresearch with Grok 4.6\nWe additionally ran Grok 4.6 with xhigh reasoning effort through the Cursor agent harness, using the same exact setup as the main experiments.\nThe run recorded 900 candidates, and accepted 44 improvements.\nIts final champion, AutoSella-G, was obtained at cycle 897 and reduced mean relative force-call cost to 97.79% on training and 98.14% on validation.\nFigure [4](#A8.F4) shows the full run, which spans 66 active research hours.\nOn the held-out GFN2-xTB benchmarks, AutoSella-G provides little benefit on single-molecule systems and performs substantially worse on the intermolecular datasets (Table [7](#A8.T7)).\nIts mean per-molecule force-call cost is 99.27% of Sella on Test and 100.49% on Dipeptides, rising to 148.77% on amino acid–ligand pairs and 239.85% on Solvated PubChem (calculated on converged optimizations).\nFor Solvated PubChem, 64 optimizations do not converge within the budget and four encounter execution errors.\nGiven this poor GFN2-xTB performance, we did not evaluate AutoSella-G with DFT and excluded it from the main-text comparisons.\nThese results underscore the performance gap between the frontier models Fable and Astra and the competing Grok 4.6 model on this complex research task.\n[1](#S3.E1))–(\n[2](#S3.E2)) and use jointly converged pairs. Err. b/a counts unsuccessful Sella/AutoSella-G runs, including non-convergence within the force-call budget.\n| GFN2-xTB |  |  |  | Relative force calls (%, Eq. ( [1](#S3.E1))) \\(\\downarrow\\) |  |  | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Benchmark | \\(N\\) | Method | Err. b/a \\(\\downarrow\\) | \\(C_{\\mathrm{pooled}}\\) | \\(C_{\\text{per-mol}}\\) | \\(\\bar{r}\\) \\(\\uparrow\\) | \n| Test | 500 | AutoSella-G | 0/1 | \\(99.05\\) | \\(99.27\\) | \\(1.000074\\) | \n| Dipeptides | 673 | AutoSella-G | 0/0 | \\(99.71\\) | \\(100.49\\) | \\(1.000449\\) | \n| AA–ligand pairs | 475 | AutoSella-G | 0/2 | \\(140.32\\) | \\(148.77\\) | \\(1.027613\\) | \n| Solvated PubChem | 100 | AutoSella-G | 2/68 | \\(216.52\\) | \\(239.85\\) | \\(0.983101\\) |", "segments": null}, {"item_id": "2c2f305856a15081f2fe9066", "source_id": "arxiv-discovery", "publisher_id": "arxiv-unresolved-team", "kind": "feed", "title": "Learning What to Investigate Next: Meta-Reasoning for Long-Horizon Research Agents", "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02525", "published_at": "2026-10-01T21:54:47Z", "retrieved_at": "2026-10-15T00:31:12.308994Z", "media_url": null, "discovered_updated_at": "2026-10-01T21:54:47Z", "discovery_method": "syndication", "version_id": "2c18cdc1f7cd0b9f4f773d6fa5733d29d115f5752e8ad9fe28d99ad61680389e", "text_path": "data/assets/2c2f305856a15081f2fe9066/2c18cdc1f7cd0b9f4f773d6fa5733d29d115f5752e8ad9fe28d99ad61680389e/text.txt", "segments_path": null, "text": "Learning What to Investigate Next: Meta-Reasoning for Long-Horizon Research Agents\nAbstract\nLong-horizon research agents must decide both how to investigate and what to investigate next as evidence accumulates. This is hard to learn because such decisions are sparse in long execution traces, and their consequences may emerge several investigations later. We introduce Meta-reasoning for Iterative Research Agents (MIRA), a hierarchical architecture separating research allocation from execution. An outer-loop meta-reasoner curates context from a persistent research record, then writes a work order for the next investigation or ends the episode. A fresh inner-loop executor carries out each work order, making execution part of the transition between meta-reasoning actions. Without policy training, MIRA improves long-horizon inference and allocates additional compute more effectively in theorem proving and open-ended neural-architecture research. Its decision boundaries also provide natural units for credit assignment. At each boundary, we train a generative critic to forecast expected remaining return from partial states, outperforming token-level alternatives. Cross-environment pretraining improves forecasting and adaptation, yielding a transferable prior for valuing partial progress. We use this prior to initialize MIRA-AC, a generative actor–critic jointly trained to forecast remaining return and choose the next investigation, without a separate critic model. MIRA-AC concentrates policy optimization on meta-reasoning decisions, enabling efficient long-horizon reinforcement learning without directly optimizing the longer execution traces they initiate. Training MIRA-AC on the model’s own proxy hill-climbing signals improves gold performance across four autoresearch environments; the actor transfers with cross-environment value initialization. Together, these results show that meta-reasoning can be learned as an explicit policy for directing long-horizon autonomous research.\n[as7416@columbia.edu](mailto:as7416@columbia.edu)), Anirudh Goyal (\n[agi@meta.com](mailto:agi@meta.com))††date: October 1, 2026\n1 Introduction\nLong-horizon research requires more than executing individual\ninvestigations well.\nAs evidence accumulates, an agent must decide which hypothesis to test,\nwhich result to verify, whether to refine or abandon a direction, and\nwhen to stop.\nThese decisions draw on evidence from many\nexperiments ([Gao et al., 2026](#bib.bib48); [Zou et al., 2026](#bib.bib27)), which can\nquickly exceed the model’s context window ([Kausik et al., 2026](#bib.bib55)). The decisions\nthemselves are sparse within much longer execution traces, and their\nconsequences may be delayed ([Sutton and Barto, 2018](#bib.bib72); [Samanta et al., 2026a](#bib.bib56)):\nan unsuccessful experiment can eliminate an important hypothesis, while\na locally promising direction can ultimately lead to a dead end.\nIn this sense, the quality of a research trajectory often depends less on the\noutcome of any single investigation than on the choices that determine what to\ninvestigate next.\nLearning to conduct research therefore requires isolating the decisions that\nallocate future research effort from the executions they initiate and\nassigning them credit for their downstream consequences. This motivates the central question of this work:\nCan a research agent learn what to investigate next from the downstream consequences of its previous research decisions?\nWe view this as a problem of meta-reasoning: deciding which\ncomputation a resource-bounded agent should perform next and when it should stop\n([Russell and Wefald, 1991](#bib.bib57)).\nFor research agents, we take the relevant computation to be an\ninvestigation.\nSec. [2](#S2) formalizes research at the level of investigations.\nSec. [3](#S3) introduces MIRA, a hierarchical architecture that separates\ndeciding what to investigate from carrying out the investigation.\nAt decision \\(t\\), the outer loop curates evidence from a persistent research\nrecord into an observation \\(o_{t}\\). The meta-reasoning policy then samples a\ncomplete decision response \\(a_{t}\\), comprising its reasoning followed by either a\nwork order or a termination decision.\nFor a work order, a fresh executor carries out the investigation and\nupdates the persistent research record.\nAt the meta-reasoning level, the potentially long executor trace is therefore compressed into one decision-level transition \\(\\xi_{t}=(o_{t},a_{t},r_{t},o_{t+1},d_{t})\\), where \\(d_{t}\\) denotes true episode termination. The complete decision response is the action; its implementation, experimentation, and tool use are part of the environment transition. This isolates the decisions that determine how future research effort is allocated from the longer computation used to execute them.\nSec. [4](#S4) develops a model of remaining research value.\nMaking research decisions explicit also creates a natural boundary for value\nprediction and credit assignment. Because an investigation’s immediate outcome\nneed not reflect its eventual contribution, we learn its expected remaining\nresearch return \\(V^{\\pi}(o_{t})=\\mathbb{E}_{\\pi}[G_{t}\\mid o_{t}]\\).\nWe train a decision-level generative critic to forecast\nthis quantity once per research decision from the same observation available\nto the policy. It uses the language model’s native next-token distribution to\nrepresent beliefs over future returns ([Zhang et al., 2025](#bib.bib61); [Bellemare et al., 2017](#bib.bib64)). The critic forecasts the future potential of\nthe current curated research observation rather than ranking candidate work orders.\nSec. [5](#S5) uses this signal to assign delayed credit to the research\npolicy. We introduce MIRA-AC, a shared generative actor–critic jointly\ntrained to forecast remaining return and choose what to investigate next from\neach curated research observation. Because one model plays both roles, no\nseparate critic model is required. MIRA-AC reconstructs complete research\nepisodes to assign delayed credit to research decisions.\nA single decision-level advantage is\npropagated to every sampled\ntoken in the corresponding decision response, including its reasoning and\nresulting work order or termination decision. Context curation, executor\ngenerations, experiments, and tool calls receive no direct policy loss.\nMIRA-AC therefore concentrates policy optimization on what to investigate\nnext without needing to directly optimize the much longer execution\ntrajectories. We train it with a buffered single-rollout asynchronous\noptimization scheme ([Hou et al., 2026](#bib.bib5)) that accommodates variable-duration research\nepisodes. Overall, we show that jointly training value forecasting and\nmeta-reasoning from the same curated research observations—while assigning credit\ndirectly to the resulting decisions—can produce a stronger policy.\nSec. [6](#S6) evaluates each stage in turn. First, untrained MIRA\nimproves long-horizon inference in theorem proving on\nIMOProofBench ([Luong et al., 2025](#bib.bib66)) and in open-ended neural-architecture\nresearch with Residual Matrix Transformers ([Mak and Flanigan, 2025](#bib.bib65)) and Loop\nTransformers ([Geiping et al., 2025](#bib.bib67)). In the latter settings, it selects\nper-cell residual-write gates for RMT and signed attention with a leaky\nrecurrent update for the Loop Transformer. Second, a decision-level generative\ncritic forecasts remaining return more accurately than token-level alternatives,\nand cross-environment pretraining improves value forecasting and adaptation.\nThird, under proxy supervision, the strongest decision-level policy-learning\nconfiguration improves the primary outcome over token-level regression across\nsymbolic regression ([Shojaee et al., 2025](#bib.bib3)), physics\ndiscovery ([Wiemann et al., 2026](#bib.bib43)), Bayesian causal discovery on\nnetworks from the Bayesian Network Repository ([Scutari, n.d.](#bib.bib2)), and\nCPU-based neural-architecture research. Crucially, training on proxy\nhill-climbing feedback collected from the model’s own research trajectories\nimproves gold-evaluation performance across all four environments. MIRA-AC\nprovides additional gains in several environments, and a policy trained without BNLearn\nactor updates transfers to BNLearn when initialized from a critic pretrained\nacross environments, including BNLearn. This separation between proxy training\nand gold evaluation mirrors autoresearch settings in which proxy evaluations\nsupport inexpensive iteration, while gold evaluation may, for example, require\nconducting in vivo experiments ([Stokes et al., 2020](#bib.bib36)) or running a large-scale\nproduction system ([Novikov et al., 2025](#bib.bib35)). Together, these results\nshow that research allocation can be effectively governed by an explicit,\nlearnable meta-reasoning policy rather than remaining an implicit byproduct of\nlong-horizon generation.\n2 Research as a Sequential Decision Process\nLong-horizon research interleaves two kinds of computation: deciding what to investigate next and carrying out the chosen investigation. Our central modeling choice is to place the learning boundary between them. One research decision is one action; when that action selects an investigation, the potentially much longer computation used to execute it is part of the environment transition.\n2.1 Decision Process\nLet \\(H_{t}\\) denote the persistent research state immediately before research decision \\(t\\). It contains the current artifact together with evidence accumulated from earlier investigations and the remaining research budget. Meta-reasoning includes both evidence curation and action selection. In this work, we hold curation fixed and train the core action-selection policy. A fixed context-curation procedure \\(C\\) constructs the observation \\(o_{t}=C(H_{t})\\) presented to that policy. Retrieval and summarization used to construct \\(o_{t}\\) belong to observation construction rather than the action.\nConditioned on \\(o_{t}\\), the policy selects \\(a_{t}\\sim\\pi_{\\theta}(\\cdot\\mid o_{t})\\), where \\(a_{t}\\) is the complete sampled meta-reasoning response: its emitted reasoning followed by either a work order or a termination decision. A work order specifies a research objective, a proposed approach, and criteria for determining whether the investigation succeeded.\nFor a work order, an executor \\(\\mathcal{E}\\) carries out the investigation. Its execution may contain many model generations, tool calls, experiments, and revisions, but remains part of a single environment transition, \\(H_{t}\\xrightarrow{a_{t},\\mathcal{E}}H_{t+1}\\).\nLet \\(d_{t}\\in\\{0,1\\}\\) indicate true termination after decision \\(t\\). For a non-terminal transition, the next observation is \\(o_{t+1}=C(H_{t+1})\\). For a terminal transition, we write \\(o_{t+1}=\\bot\\) and use the convention \\(V(\\bot)=0\\). A termination decision is itself a terminal research action.\nAt the meta-reasoning level, the stored transition is therefore \\(\\xi_{t}:=(o_{t},a_{t},r_{t},o_{t+1},d_{t})\\). Context resets, executor restarts, and learner-batch boundaries do not set \\(d_{t}=1\\).\nWriting \\(T=\\min\\{t:d_{t}=1\\}\\), a logical research episode is \\(\\tau=(\\xi_{1},\\ldots,\\xi_{T})\\).\nThus, the unit of control is the research decision: the policy chooses what to investigate or whether to terminate, while the executor determines how a selected investigation is carried out.\n2.2 Research Feedback and Return\nOur environments expose proxy feedback during research and apply a separate gold evaluation only after final submission. The gold evaluator reports the task-specific final metrics and is never available to the agent during the research episode.\nProxy feedback.\nLet \\(q_{t}\\in[0,1]\\) be the normalized proxy quality after decision \\(t\\), with \\(q_{0}\\) the initial quality, and define \\(\\bar{q}_{t}=\\max_{0\\leq u\\leq t}q_{u}\\). The proxy reward is the improvement in the incumbent, \\(r_{t}^{\\mathrm{proxy}}=\\bar{q}_{t}-\\bar{q}_{t-1}\\). Hence the remaining proxy return telescopes: \\(G_{t}^{\\mathrm{proxy}}=\\sum_{u=t}^{T}r_{u}^{\\mathrm{proxy}}=\\bar{q}_{T}-\\bar{q}_{t-1}\\). Each environment uses a monotone proxy normalization fixed before training. The proxy and gold evaluations need not rank artifacts identically.\nGold-binary feedback.\nGold evaluation may contain continuous metrics as well as an environment-specific binary success criterion. For the Gold binary training condition, let \\(Y\\in\\{0,1\\}\\) denote this terminal success outcome. Intermediate rewards are zero and the terminal reward is \\(Y\\), so \\(G_{t}^{\\mathrm{gold\\text{-}bin}}=Y\\).\nFor either feedback signal, we write \\(G_{t}=\\sum_{u=t}^{T}r_{u}\\) for the\nundiscounted remaining return.\nEnvironment-specific definitions of \\(q_{t}\\) and \\(Y\\) are given in\nApp. [B](#A2).\n2.3 Remaining Research Value\nFor a fixed continuation policy and research budget, we define \\(V^{\\pi}(o_{t})=\\mathbb{E}_{\\pi}[G_{t}\\mid o_{t}]\\). Because \\(o_{t}\\) is a curated observation, this is an observation-conditioned value rather than a value of the complete research state. Under proxy feedback, \\(V^{\\pi}(o_{t})\\) is the expected additional best-so-far improvement from continuing research. Under gold-binary feedback, it is the probability of success under the continuation policy. It is not the maximum improvement theoretically attainable from the underlying research state.\nThe remainder of the method instantiates this decision process: MIRA exposes the research actions, a critic forecasts remaining value from the resulting observations, and MIRA-AC assigns downstream credit to the policy that selects them.\n3 MIRA: Exposing Meta-Reasoning Decisions\nThe decision-level formulation in Sec. [2](#S2) requires an\nagent architecture that exposes individual investigations as explicit actions\nwhile preserving evidence across a much longer research trajectory.\nIn particular, the system must maintain research state across investigations,\nseparate the choice of what to investigate from the computation used to carry\nit out, and produce a well-defined decision boundary at which outcomes can\nlater be valued and credited.\nMIRA realizes these requirements with a persistent research substrate, an\nouter-loop meta-reasoner, and a fresh inner-loop executor.\n3.1 MIRA Architecture\nSeparating research allocation from execution requires evidence to persist across investigations. We thus introduce MIRA, a hierarchical research harness in which an outer-loop meta-reasoner and an inner-loop executor are instantiated afresh at each iteration over a persistent research substrate.\nPersistent research substrate.\nEach task has a Git repository that persists throughout the research episode. It stores the current artifact, prior experiments and their outcomes, analyses, code or proof artifacts, and handovers from earlier executors. The repository implements the persistent research state \\(H_{t}\\); model contexts are temporary.\nOuter-loop meta-reasoner.\nAt each decision boundary, the context-curation procedure constructs \\(o_{t}\\) from the persistent record. The meta-reasoning policy then samples the complete decision response \\(a_{t}\\): its emitted reasoning followed by either a work order or a termination decision. A work order specifies the next objective, proposed approach, and success criteria. The outer loop therefore allocates the next unit of research effort.\nFresh inner-loop executor.\nEach work order launches a fresh execution agent through the Codex\nharness ([OpenAI, 2025](#bib.bib74)), with the underlying model specified by the\nexperiment. It receives the fixed task instructions, the work order, and access\nto the persistent workspace and task-specific tools. The executor performs the investigation,\nupdates the artifact, and commits a handover recording its evidence and\nunresolved questions. Both outer- and inner-loop contexts reset between\ndecisions; only the persistent research substrate survives.\nFig. [1](#S1.F1) illustrates this separation between research\nallocation and execution.\n3.2 MIRA Research Procedure\nAlgorithm [1](#alg1) formalizes this research procedure. Each outer-loop decision produces exactly one\ndecision-level transition, regardless of the length of the corresponding\nexecutor trace. The shared procedure and executor interface remain fixed across\ntasks; App. [C](#A3) gives the context construction,\nevaluation, and submission details.\n3.3 Learning Boundary\nMIRA’s architectural separation also determines where MIRA-AC applies learning. It optimizes the policy that generates decision responses and the critic that forecasts remaining return, while holding curation, execution, and the task environment fixed.\n| Component | Role | Trained? | \n|---|---|---|\n| Curator \\(C\\) | Constructs \\(o_{t}\\) | No | \n| Policy \\(\\pi_{\\theta}\\) | Generates reasoning and work order / termination | Yes | \n| Value critic | Forecasts remaining return | Yes | \n| Executor \\(\\mathcal{E}\\) | Implements investigation | No | \n| Tools / evaluators | Environment interaction | No | \n| Git substrate \\(H_{t}\\) | Persistent research state | — | \n4 Learning to Forecast the Future Value of Research Progress\nAn investigation’s immediate outcome need not reflect its eventual contribution. A failed experiment may eliminate an incorrect hypothesis and enable later progress, while an immediate improvement may lead to a dead end. We therefore learn a value function \\(V^{\\pi}(o_{t})\\) at MIRA’s research-decision boundaries.\nThe environments report progress in different raw units, so we use the gold\nand proxy returns defined in Sec. [2.2](#S2.SS2). Each environment maps its\nproxy metric through a fixed environment-specific transform to a quality in\n\\([0,1]\\).\nThese transforms are scaled to distinguish improvements attainable within the\nresearch budget and convert partial trajectories into marginal rewards.\nApp. [B.2](#A2.SS2) describes their anchors, relation to gold\nevaluation, and construction for new environments.\n4.1 Decision-Level Generative Critic\nThe critic produces one value forecast immediately before each research action. This aligns value forecasting with the unit of control, rather than with intermediate token prefixes inside the language-model generation of a decision response.\nWe parameterize the critic using the language model’s next-token distribution.\nFollowing [Samanta et al. (2026b)](#bib.bib63), we elicit its beliefs by renormalizing\nthe next-token log probabilities assigned to a set of single-token labels, each\nrepresenting a possible future-return value. Let\n\\(\\mathcal{Z}=\\{z_{1}<\\cdots<z_{K}\\}\\) denote these values. For observation \\(o_{t}\\),\n\\(p_{\\phi}(z_{i}\\mid o_{t})=\\exp\\ell_{\\phi,i}(o_{t})/\\sum_{k=1}^{K}\\exp\\ell_{\\phi,k}(o_{t})\\), and\n\\(V_{\\phi}(o_{t})=\\sum_{i=1}^{K}p_{\\phi}(z_{i}\\mid o_{t})z_{i}\\).\nWe use support \\(\\{0.0,0.1,\\ldots,1.0\\}\\) for proxy returns and \\(\\{0,1\\}\\) for gold-binary outcomes. The critic forecasts expected remaining return under the continuation policy; it does not estimate maximum attainable improvement or score candidate work orders.\n4.2 Critic Objective\nThe training target is the Monte Carlo return \\(G_{t}\\) from the complete research episode. For \\(z_{j}<G_{t}<z_{j+1}\\), we project the target onto its neighboring support points by setting \\(y_{j}(G_{t})=(z_{j+1}-G_{t})/(z_{j+1}-z_{j})\\) and \\(y_{j+1}(G_{t})=(G_{t}-z_{j})/(z_{j+1}-z_{j})\\), with all other components zero. If \\(G_{t}=z_{j}\\), then \\(y_{j}(G_{t})=1\\). The projection preserves the target expectation.\nFor a batch \\(\\mathcal{B}\\), the critic minimizes\n|  | \\[\\mathcal{L}_{\\mathrm{critic}}(\\phi)=\\frac{1}{\\|\\mathcal{B}\\|}\\sum_{t\\in\\mathcal{B}}D_{\\mathrm{KL}}\\left(y(G_{t})\\,\\\\|\\,p_{\\phi}(\\cdot\\mid o_{t})\\right).\\] |  | (1) | \nOperationally, we select the model’s next-token log probabilities for the \\(K\\) support labels and renormalize them over that support with a log-softmax. We then evaluate the KL objective above; its parameter-dependent term is \\(-\\sum_{i}y_{i}(G_{t})\\log p_{\\phi}(z_{i}\\mid o_{t})\\), because the target entropy is constant with respect to \\(\\phi\\).\n4.3 Cross-Environment Value Pretraining\nResearch environments differ in artifacts, tools, and objectives, but partial research observations may share predictive structure. For a target environment \\(d\\in\\mathcal{D}\\), leave-one-environment-out (LOO) pretraining uses trajectories from the source set \\(\\mathcal{D}\\setminus\\{d\\}\\), after which the critic is adapted on target-environment support episodes and evaluated on held-out target query episodes. The experiments below compare this initialization with target-only and single-source training.\nValue forecasting alone does not change the research policy. The next section uses downstream outcomes to assign credit to the decisions that select what to investigate.\n5 MIRA-AC: Learning What to Investigate Next\nWe now ask whether value estimates at MIRA’s decision boundaries can improve the policy that selects research objectives. We introduce MIRA-AC, a shared generative actor–critic that assigns delayed credit across complete research episodes and uses buffered asynchronous optimization to accommodate their variable durations.\n5.1 Shared Actor and Critic\nMIRA-AC uses one language model with shared parameters \\(\\theta\\). In actor mode\nit represents \\(\\pi_{\\theta}\\); in critic mode its restricted next-token\ndistribution defines \\(V_{\\theta}\\) as in Sec. [4.1](#S4.SS1).\nThe shared model is initialized from a pretrained critic checkpoint. Actor and critic objectives use separate optimizer states while updating the shared model. Thus, unlike a separate actor–critic, value learning also updates representations used to generate subsequent research decisions.\n5.2 Credit Assignment Across Research Decisions\nMIRA-AC reconstructs each logical episode \\(\\tau\\) before computing learning targets. Context resets and learner-batch boundaries may partition an episode in storage, but only \\(d_{t}=1\\) stops value propagation.\nThe critic is trained on the exact return \\(G_{t}\\). For actor training, we use\ngeneralized advantage estimation ([Schulman et al., 2016](#bib.bib70)) with \\(\\gamma=1\\) and\n\\(\\lambda_{T}=1-1/(\\alpha T)\\), with the fixed hyperparameter \\(\\alpha\\) given in\nTable [3](#A8.T3).\nBecause asynchronous collection allows successive decisions in one episode to use different critic versions, let \\(c_{t}\\) denote the critic version used at decision \\(t\\). At rollout time we record the current baseline \\(v_{t}=V_{\\theta^{c_{t}}}(o_{t})\\) and same-version successor bootstrap \\(\\widetilde{v}_{t+1}^{(c_{t})}=V_{\\theta^{c_{t}}}(o_{t+1})\\). The temporal-difference residual is \\(\\delta_{t}=r_{t}+\\gamma(1-d_{t})\\widetilde{v}_{t+1}^{(c_{t})}-v_{t}\\).\nAt a non-terminal boundary where the critic version changes, define \\(B_{t}=v_{t+1}-\\widetilde{v}_{t+1}^{(c_{t})}\\), where \\(v_{t+1}=V_{\\theta^{c_{t+1}}}(o_{t+1})\\); otherwise \\(B_{t}=0\\). Advantages are computed backward as\n|  | \\[\\hat{A}_{t}=\\delta_{t}+\\gamma\\lambda_{T}(1-d_{t})\\left(\\hat{A}_{t+1}+B_{t}\\right).\\] |  | (2) | \nThe bridge aligns critic baselines across model versions; it does not modify\nthe Monte Carlo target. App. [I](#A9) gives the complete\nepisode-reconstruction details.\n5.3 Decision-Level Actor Objective\nAlthough each research action receives one decision-level advantage, the language model generates it autoregressively. Let \\(a_{t,j}\\) denote token \\(j\\) of action \\(a_{t}\\), let \\(L_{t}\\) be its sampled length, and let \\(b_{t}\\) denote the behavior-policy version that generated it. Define \\(\\rho_{t,j}=\\pi_{\\theta}(a_{t,j}\\mid o_{t},a_{t,<j})/\\pi_{\\theta^{b_{t}}}(a_{t,j}\\mid o_{t},a_{t,<j})\\). We mask policy-gradient terms outside the allowed importance-ratio range using \\(m_{t,j}=\\mathbf{1}[1-\\epsilon_{\\mathrm{low}}<\\rho_{t,j}<1+\\epsilon_{\\mathrm{high}}]\\).\nFor a batch of \\(N\\) research decisions, the actor objective is\n|  | \\[\\begin{split}\\mathcal{L}_{\\mathrm{actor}}(\\theta)=\\frac{1}{N}\\sum_{t=1}^{N}\\frac{1}{L_{t}}\\sum_{j=1}^{L_{t}}\\left[-m_{t,j}\\tau_{A}\\hat{A}_{t}\\rho_{t,j}+\\tau_{\\mathrm{KL}}(\\log\\rho_{t,j})^{2}\\right].\\end{split}\\] |  | (3) | \nTokens outside the ratio interval are excluded from the policy-gradient term but remain in the policy-mismatch penalty. The trainable span is the complete sampled meta-reasoning decision response, including its emitted reasoning and resulting work order or termination decision. Prompts, curated observations, executor generations, tool calls, and experiments are masked.\n5.4 Buffered Single-Rollout Asynchronous Policy Optimization\nInvestigations have heterogeneous wall-clock durations, so synchronized rollout cohorts can leave capacity idle. MIRA-AC instead collects completed episodes continuously into separate actor and critic buffers, which trigger updates independently on the shared model. If both are ready, the critic update is applied first, followed by the actor update within the same atomic update transaction. The resulting parameters are published once. A live episode adopts newly published parameters only at its next research-decision boundary and may therefore span multiple model versions.\nAlgorithm [2](#alg2) summarizes training. Buffer thresholds, optimizer\nsettings, rollout limits, and details of low-rank adaptation\n(LoRA) ([Hu et al., 2022](#bib.bib71)) are given in\nApp. [H](#A8).\n6 Experiments\nWe evaluate the approach in four stages. First, we ask whether MIRA improves long-horizon inference before policy training. Second, we test whether remaining research value can be forecast at MIRA’s decision boundaries and whether this forecasting capability transfers across research environments. Third, we evaluate whether decision-level credit improves the research policy and whether the learned policy transfers across environments. Finally, we analyze how training changes research behavior and how MIRA’s decision-level decomposition affects where policy learning is applied and the cost of collecting long-horizon trajectories.\nThese stages place different demands on the experimental setting. To evaluate MIRA before learning, we use full-scale long-horizon settings in theorem proving and open-ended neural-architecture research. Value pretraining and policy learning, however, require many complete research trajectories. We therefore evaluate the learning components in scalable autoresearch environments that support repeated training and held-out evaluation.\n6.1 Does MIRA Improve Long-Horizon Inference Without Policy Training?\nExperimental setup.\nAll comparisons in this subsection use frozen model weights. We compare GPT-5.5,\nCodex, and MIRA.\nGPT-5.5 uses the task interface directly; Codex operates as one persistent\ntool-using session; and MIRA assigns each investigation to a fresh Codex inner\nloop. The Codex and MIRA conditions use the same underlying\nGPT-5.5 model, Codex harness, and tools, while MIRA adds explicit meta-reasoning\nand curated context resets.\nWe test the three conditions on IMOProofBench ([Luong et al., 2025](#bib.bib66))\nand HillClimbBench-RMT, and compare MIRA with\nCodex on HillClimbBench-Loop. For theorem\nproving, we also include output-token-matched\ncontinuation controls.\nTheorem proving.\nOn IMOProofBench Advanced, we count a problem as solved only when the grader\nawards full credit. Using GPT-5.5, MIRA raises the\nmean normalized grader score from 67.1% under direct inference\nto 100%, solving all 30 problems in\neach of three trials. With Qwen3.6-27B ([Qwen Team, 2026](#bib.bib73)),\nMIRA also more than doubles\nthe model’s score\n(Fig. [4](#S6.F4)(a)).\nAll conditions receive the same runtime budget, but MIRA makes better use of\nits inference budget to iterate on its solution, while the baselines submit\nmuch earlier. To determine whether its gains come from this allocation or\nsimply from generating more tokens, we force the baselines to continue until\nthey match MIRA’s output-token count on each problem. This recovers\nmost of the performance gap, but the residual gap\nis concentrated on four problems. On these\nproblems, GPT-5.5 and Codex primarily extend or repair\nexisting proof directions, whereas MIRA more often\nrevisits earlier evidence and explores alternatives.\nWe characterize this behavior using strategic\ninertia, the fraction of consecutive semantic\nsteps within the same research direction. Across\nnatural-stopping runs using GPT-5.5, mean inertia is\n0.958 for direct inference, 0.862 for Codex, and\n0.772 for MIRA. MIRA’s lower inertia means that it switches between proof\ndirections more often.\nFig. [5](#S6.F5)\nillustrates this behavior on Problem 23;\nApp. [D](#A4) provides the\nmetric definition and additional examples.\nNeural-architecture research.\nWe introduce HillClimbBench to evaluate MIRA in\nopen-ended experimental research. Each environment starts\nfrom a task’s paper-derived reference architecture\nand asks the agent to improve it through code\nchanges and experiments. We study\ntwo architecture-research tasks; Sec. [5](#S5) uses a separate\n50-task collection, HillClimbBench-CPU, for policy learning. Each task provides\nproxy evaluations that the agent can use to hill-climb during research. After\nsubmission, a held-out gold evaluation scores the final design.\nResidual Matrix Transformer.\nThe Residual Matrix Transformer (RMT) ([Mak and Flanigan, 2025](#bib.bib65)) replaces\nthe usual vector-valued residual stream of the Transformer\narchitecture ([Vaswani et al., 2017](#bib.bib69)) with a matrix that each layer reads\nfrom and writes to. The research problem is to regulate where attention and MLP\nupdates write into this state without discarding its matrix structure.\nHillClimbBench-RMT asks the agent to improve the architecture under that\nconstraint.\nWe run matched 24-hour GPT-5.5 campaigns under the three orchestration\nconditions. GPT-5.5 stays close to conventional\ntraining and scaling choices, while Codex refines an\nRMT-specific normalization family. After more elaborate\nrouting mechanisms prove ineffective at scale, MIRA shifts\nfrom normalization and cross-axis mixing to local write\ncontrol and selects learned per-cell residual-write gates.\nMIRA achieves the lowest proxy and\ngold loss among the three campaigns\n(Fig. [4](#S6.F4)(b)).\nApp. [E](#A5) provides the matched controls, architecture,\ninitialization, and ablations.\nLoop Transformer.\nA Loop Transformer ([Geiping et al., 2025](#bib.bib67)) repeatedly applies a shared\nblock, increasing effective depth without\nintroducing distinct parameters at every\niteration. Its central challenge is to revise the recurrent state without\nerasing information needed by later applications of the block.\nHillClimbBench-Loop asks the agent to improve this information flow.\nWe compare matched 48-hour MIRA and Codex\ncampaigns using GPT-6 Astra. MIRA first hypothesizes that repeated core\napplications overwrite useful state. When an adaptive retention gate does not\nbeat constant interpolation with the previous state, it retains the simpler\nleaky update. After memory and routing alternatives produce small or\ninconsistent gains, MIRA hypothesizes that repeated positive attention may\nreinforce information that later recurrences struggle to remove. It arrives at\nwhat it calls signed attention, which subtracts one attention read over\nshared values from another, and tests it against a matched positive-mixture\ncontrol before scaling. This resembles a fixed-coefficient variant of\nDifferential Attention ([Ye et al., 2025](#bib.bib68)) applied in a Loop\nTransformer.\nMIRA achieves lower NLL than Codex under both proxy and gold evaluation. Both\ncampaigns also outperform the task reference architecture on the proxy metric.\nThe selected RMT and Loop designs both regulate updates to persistent model\nstate: the RMT design scales how strongly attention and MLP branches write to\neach matrix cell, while the Loop design allows recurrent attention to suppress\nas well as reinforce information and preserves part of the previous state.\nFig. [6](#S6.F6) shows the\nprogression of both research campaigns;\nApp. [F](#A6) details the matched\ncontrols, operator, initialization, and selected designs.\nContext curation.\nAcross the Loop campaign, MIRA retains a stable task scaffold while selecting\nthe notes, code, and measurements relevant to the active experiment. Recent\nartifacts and the latest handover preserve continuity across context resets,\nwhile the curated context contracts after final candidate selection\n(Fig. [7](#S6.F7)).\nThese studies show how MIRA reallocates inference as evidence accumulates. Making each meta-reasoning decision explicit also provides a natural boundary for forecasting remaining progress and assigning credit from later outcomes.\n6.2 Can We Forecast Remaining Research Value?\nUsing proxy-marginal returns, we compare three decision-level critics—scalar, categorical-head, and generative—with scalar and generative token-level baselines. For each environment, we form five support/query splits, each with task-disjoint sets of 50 support and 50 query episodes. Under matched conditions, each critic starts from the same Qwen-27B initialization and trains on all 50 support episodes. We evaluate it on the query episodes using mean per-episode root mean squared error (RMSE). For query episodes \\(\\mathcal{Q}\\), this is\n|  | \\[\\mathrm{RMSE}=\\frac{1}{\\|\\mathcal{Q}\\|}\\sum_{\\tau\\in\\mathcal{Q}}\\sqrt{\\frac{1}{T_{\\tau}}\\sum_{t=1}^{T_{\\tau}}\\left(V_{\\phi}(o_{t})-G_{t}\\right)^{2}}.\\] |  | (4) | \nThe decision-level generative critic achieves the\nlowest mean held-out RMSE among the tested designs,\nreducing RMSE by an absolute 0.110\nrelative to its token-level generative counterpart\n(Fig. [8](#S6.F8)(a)).\nWe use this design in the subsequent transfer\nand policy-learning experiments.\nWe test whether critic pretraining improves\nforecasting in an unseen research environment.\nFor each target environment, we compare a target-only\nmodel with models initialized by pretraining on one source\nenvironment or on all remaining environments.\nThe latter is the LOO initialization defined above. We then adapt each critic on\n0, 1, 5, 10, 25, or 50\ntarget-environment support episodes and evaluate it on the same 50 held-out\nquery episodes. This measures how quickly forecasting improves from each\ninitialization as target-environment data increases.\nAcross environments, LOO initialization enables better zero-shot forecasting\nand faster adaptation than either single-source pretraining or no pretraining.\nAfter adaptation\non five target-environment episodes, it reduces RMSE\nby an absolute 0.053 compared with target-only training\n(Fig. [8](#S6.F8)(b)). We use pretrained critics to assign\ndelayed credit and train MIRA’s research-decision\npolicy.\n6.3 Does Decision-Level Credit Improve the Research Policy?\nExperimental protocol.\nWe evaluate on four environments: BNLearn causal discovery, constructed from\nthe Bayesian Network Repository ([Scutari, n.d.](#bib.bib2)); Physics\nDiscovery ([Wiemann et al., 2026](#bib.bib43)), SRBench symbolic\nregression ([Shojaee et al., 2025](#bib.bib3)), and HillClimbBench-CPU. The first\nthree each use 100 tasks.\nHillClimbBench-CPU has 50 paper-derived tasks,\neach with two independently initialized rollouts.\nQwen-27B is the underlying model for every MIRA agent role. In trained\nconfigurations, the learned checkpoint is used only for action selection and value\nforecasting; curation and execution remain fixed. We organize the comparison as an ablation ladder over\nsupervision, credit granularity, critic parameterization,\nand actor–critic co-training. We begin with a\nseparate actor–critic that uses token-level scalar regression\nand gold-binary outcomes. This Gold binary / Token regression condition\nis our analogue of single-rollout asynchronous methods such as\nSAO ([Hou et al., 2026](#bib.bib5)), which typically optimize all rollout tokens. Restricting the\nactor loss to the complete sampled MIRA decision response—its reasoning plus work order or\ntermination decision—tests the same approach for learning a meta-reasoning\npolicy. Holding the critic\nparameterization and granularity fixed, we replace terminal\nfeedback with marginal improvements in the proxy metric.\nUnder proxy supervision, i.e., training on proxy returns rather than the\ngold-binary outcome, we then move value\nforecasting to MIRA’s decision boundaries using decision-level scalar\nregression (Step regression) and replace scalar regression with the\ndecision-level generative critic (Step generative). We evaluate the\ngenerative critic under both supervision\nregimes, first with separate policy and critic parameters and\nthen with MIRA-AC training.\nToken regression repeats the decision return target at each token prefix within the meta-reasoning action; the decision-level regression and generative critics forecast once per MIRA decision. In the separate-critic variants, the actor starts from the base policy and the critic from its pretrained checkpoint. MIRA-AC initializes the shared actor–critic from the pretrained critic checkpoint. Accordingly, this comparison evaluates the combined MIRA-AC design, not parameter sharing alone. All comparisons hold the MIRA harness, executor, tools, research horizon, inference budgets, and environment feedback fixed. Each environment-specific run makes one pass over a fixed, ordered set of 100 training episodes. The cross-environment run uses 300 episodes, comprising 100 from each of its three actor-training environments. We evaluate each run’s final policy checkpoint using gold evaluation.\n[B](#A2). Gold binary and Proxy marginal denote training feedback, not evaluation. Italics mark Gold-binary group bests, bold marks Proxy-marginal group bests, and boxes mark the best overall counts. The final row trains one MIRA-AC policy across all environments except BNLearn, which is held out from actor training but included in critic pretraining.\n|  |  | Symbolic Regression |  | Physics Discovery |  | HillClimbBench-CPU |  | BNLearn |  | \n|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|\n|  | Training variant | Solved | \\(R^{2}\\) | Solved | Law qual. | Wins | Arch. qual. | Solved | SHD gain | \n|  | MIRA (untrained) | 50 | 0.9864 | 8 | 0.2118 | — | 0.7624 | 76 | 0.9672 | \n| Gold binary | Token regression | 55 | 0.9957 | 9 | 0.2327 | 49 | 0.7594 | 79 | 0.9567 | \n|  | Step generative | 58 | 0.9993 | 9 | 0.2733 | 43 | 0.7518 | 82 | 0.9804 | \n|  | MIRA-AC | 65 | 0.9879 | 6 | 0.1846 | 49 | 0.7481 | 56 | 0.9261 | \n| Proxy marginal | Token regression | 55 | 0.9860 | 6 | 0.2463 | 49 | 0.7618 | 63 | 0.9002 | \n|  | Step regression | 50 | 0.9980 | 6 | 0.3043 | 46 | 0.7578 | 84 | 0.9733 | \n|  | Step generative | 59 | 0.9990 | 8 | 0.2463 | 53 | 0.7693 | 76 | 0.9106 | \n|  | MIRA-AC | 60 | 0.9994 | 10 | 0.2955 | 58 | 0.8054 | 48 | 0.9059 | \n|  | Online actor training across all environments except BNLearn |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  | \n|  | MIRA-AC | 63 | 0.9869 | 10 | 0.3028 | 55 | 0.7853 | 84 | 0.9869 | \nTraining with decision-level credit assignment improves long-horizon research.\nMIRA-AC shows that long-horizon research can be improved by assigning credit directly to the meta-reasoning decisions that shape a trajectory. Trained from proxy hill-climbing feedback across multiple autoresearch environments, the policy achieves higher gold-evaluation point estimates than untrained MIRA in all four environments. Among the separate actor–critic ablations under proxy-marginal supervision, the strongest decision-level variant improves the primary outcome over Token regression in every environment. Step generative performs best on Symbolic Regression, Physics Discovery, and HillClimbBench-CPU, while Step regression performs best on BNLearn. MIRA exposes each meta-reasoning decision, and MIRA-AC applies policy loss to its complete sampled response while masking the much longer executor trace. MIRA-AC therefore improves research outcomes without directly optimizing the much longer executor traces, demonstrating the leverage of targeted credit assignment at meta-reasoning boundaries.\nJointly learning to forecast partial progress and act from the same curated research observations produces a stronger policy.\nOrdinarily, the critic affects the policy only through the advantages used in the actor loss. Unlike Step generative, which uses separate actor and critic adapters, MIRA-AC applies both objectives to the same model with shared parameters. The critic loss therefore directly trains the policy’s representation to recognize evidence predictive of future progress. Under proxy-marginal supervision, the environment-specific MIRA-AC policy further improves the primary outcome on the three environments where Step generative is strongest. BNLearn instead favors Step regression. The critic loss provides an outcome-supervised auxiliary objective for learning what to investigate next.\nMIRA-AC demonstrates the viability of training a general meta-reasoning policy.\nBeyond the environment-specific ablations, we train a single MIRA-AC policy from\nproxy hill-climbing feedback across Symbolic Regression, Physics Discovery, and\nHillClimbBench-CPU. The cross-environment policy matches or exceeds the strongest\nseparate-adapter primary outcome in every environment. On BNLearn, it matches\nStep regression without receiving BNLearn actor updates. This experiment\nbuilds on the transferable critic prior established in\nSec. [6.2](#S6.SS2): MIRA-AC starts from a critic pretrained on all four\nenvironments, including BNLearn, to forecast progress from curated research\nobservations. Its BNLearn result therefore provides evidence that the meta-reasoning\npolicy learned from proxy feedback in the other environments transfers to\nBNLearn. Together, these results show that MIRA-AC can be scaled to a general\nmeta-reasoning policy that learns reusable research strategies for deciding\nwhat to investigate across a variety of research domains.\n6.4 What Changes After Meta-Reasoning Policy Training?\nBeyond final task outcomes, we analyze how policy training changes research behavior, which generated tokens receive actor loss, and how context curation affects rollout input costs.\nAnalysis of trained meta-reasoning trajectories reveals notable shifts in research behavior, particularly a greater tendency to target unresolved uncertainty.\nWe use an LLM judge to compare 400 matched trajectory pairs while hiding policy identities, using a rubric covering hypothesis revision, experiment choice, evidence use, robustness testing, and stopping decisions. The judge first evaluates research behavior without seeing final outcomes. After outcomes are revealed, it assesses which policy’s outer-loop behavior contributed more strongly to the corresponding trajectory’s gold outcome. It favors MIRA-AC in 203 pairs and untrained MIRA in 131, with 66 tied or unclear; among clear attributions, 60.8% favor MIRA-AC.\nAcross environments, the judge more often identifies MIRA-AC trajectories that\nrevise explanations, test competing ideas, check whether gains persist, and\nabandon stalled lines of inquiry.\nThe largest shifts are an increase in trajectories containing a test that\ndistinguishes competing ideas, from 16.8% to 28.0%, and a decrease in\ntrajectories repeatedly trying minor variants of an unproductive idea, from\n33.5% to 18.8% (Fig. [9](#S6.F9)).\nIn Symbolic Regression, this appears as changing the functional form in\nresponse to structured errors rather than continuing to tune coefficients.\nIn Physics Discovery, it appears as choosing experiments that vary one factor\nto distinguish competing physical laws.\nIn HillClimbBench-CPU, it appears as leaving a plateaued sequence of local\nmodifications for an architectural change tied to the diagnosed bottleneck.\nIn BNLearn, which receives no actor updates, the transferred policy targets\nspecific unresolved edges before expanding to broader graph search.\nApp. [K](#A11) provides matched case analyses and\ntrajectory excerpts, including cases where these changes help or hurt final\nperformance.\nActor updates target only the research-decision responses.\nAcross the four environment-specific training runs, comprising 400 episodes,\ncomplete sampled meta-reasoning decision responses account for 25.8% of\naccounted decision and execution output tokens and are the only generated tokens that\nreceive actor loss. Context-curation output is excluded because it was not\nfully logged.\nFig. [10](#S6.F10) shows this separation within one\ncomplete episode and across the full training set.\nCurated context limits repeated-history input.\nAt each research decision, MIRA reads selected evidence from the persistent\nrecord instead of replaying the entire preceding trajectory.\nUnder our accounting, MIRA-AC processes an average of 10.6 million input tokens\nper episode across the 400 training episodes.\nWe compare this recorded cost with fixed-trajectory projections that replay the\nsame trajectories while carrying prior context either in full or with\nauto-compaction at 200k tokens.\nBoth replay alternatives require more input under this projection as research\nhistory accumulates (Fig. [11](#S6.F11)).\nApp. [H](#A8) details the accounting and projection\nassumptions.\n7 Limitations and Future Work\nMIRA-AC learns only part of the meta-reasoning process. The policy that generates research decisions is trained, while evidence curation and execution remain fixed. This makes learning tractable and provides a clean credit-assignment boundary, but it also means that the policy can act only on evidence surfaced by the curator and can improve outcomes only through investigations the executor is capable of carrying out. Relevant evidence omitted during curation cannot be recovered by the actor or critic, and improvements in the executor may change which research decisions are valuable. An important next step is therefore to learn curation, allocation, and possibly execution jointly while preserving decision boundaries for credit assignment.\nThe behavioral results raise a broader question: what should a general meta-reasoner learn about how to conduct research? Policy training shapes not only task performance, but the model’s research temperament—how readily it abandons an incumbent, gathers more evidence, or commits to a larger change. In HillClimbBench-CPU, MIRA-AC’s willingness to pursue substantial architectural changes can escape local optima that trap untrained MIRA on some tasks, yet the same willingness can backfire when preserving and patiently refining the incumbent is better. The calibration problem recurs elsewhere: a new experiment is useful while physical laws remain ambiguous but wasteful once they are resolved; a richer symbolic expression can improve proxy fit while harming extrapolation; and broad graph search can be inferior to a one-edge repair. The objective is therefore not maximal exploration or aggressiveness, but context-sensitive judgment about when to persist, refine, test, pivot, or stop.\nThe environments used for policy training cover only a small fraction of the diversity required to learn this judgment. A general policy will require a deliberately constructed training distribution spanning distinct research regimes—exploration and exploitation, bold pivots and incremental refinement, continued investigation and stopping—so that the model learns not one research style, but which style and granularity of investigation a situation warrants. In this work, we demonstrate that decision-level training improves outcomes, changes research behavior, and transfers to an environment held out from actor training, providing a roadmap for scaling toward that goal.\nWe demonstrate that meta-reasoning updates transfer to an environment held out from actor training: MIRA-AC improves on BNLearn despite receiving no BNLearn actor updates. The model is, however, exposed to BNLearn during value pretraining. Future work should examine broader transfer to environments excluded from both value pretraining and actor training.\nOur experiments use a single MIRA configuration with the same Codex-based inner-loop harness across research environments. This holds the research interface fixed while varying the task. Training across heterogeneous and evolving inner-loop scaffolds is a natural next step for testing whether learned meta-reasoning remains useful as execution capabilities change.\nOur learning experiments also depend on proxy feedback. Dense proxy signals make repeated autoresearch training feasible, but a proxy need not rank research directions in the same way as the final gold evaluation. Our results show that proxy-trained policies can improve gold-evaluated outcomes in the environments studied here, but they do not establish when such transfer should be expected or how robust learning is to systematically misaligned proxies. Future work should characterize the degree of proxy–gold alignment required for stable policy improvement and develop methods that can learn from sparse gold feedback without giving up the sample efficiency of intermediate signals.\nThe behavioral analysis should likewise be interpreted as descriptive. Its conclusions rely on an LLM judge assigning process labels and comparing matched trajectories. These annotations provide evidence that training changes observable research behavior, but they do not establish that any individual behavior—such as testing competing hypotheses, abandoning stalled directions, or making larger pivots—causes the associated performance difference. More direct interventions on research strategy, together with human or task-specific behavioral evaluation, would be needed to identify which learned behaviors are causally useful and under which research regimes.\nFinally, the current optimization results are limited by scale. We train LoRA adapters rather than all model parameters, and our asynchronous procedure is evaluated only at investigation durations supported by the present infrastructure. We have not tested episodes in which individual investigations last hours or days and a single trajectory spans many more actor and critic versions. At those timescales, delayed and heterogeneous feedback, policy staleness, and interference between actor and critic updates to shared parameters may become substantially more important. Scaling to full-parameter training and longer-lived research episodes will therefore require studying update scheduling, value calibration under changing policies, and stable joint optimization over much larger temporal gaps.\nMore broadly, MIRA-AC assumes that research can be decomposed into meaningful decision boundaries at which accumulated evidence is summarized, future value can be estimated, and credit can be assigned. The environments studied here support that decomposition. Whether the same abstraction remains effective for less structured research, where useful investigations overlap, branch, or evolve continuously, is an open question. Testing that boundary is important for determining how far decision-level meta-reasoning can scale toward more general autonomous research.\n8 Conclusion\nThis work asks whether a research agent can learn what to investigate next from the downstream consequences of its previous research decisions. Across the settings studied here, our results support a positive answer. MIRA represents long-horizon research as a sequence of explicit meta-reasoning decisions over a persistent research record, treating the potentially much longer execution of each investigation as part of an environment transition. This decomposition improves long-horizon inference even before policy training and provides a natural boundary at which remaining research value can be forecast and delayed outcomes assigned to earlier decisions.\nBuilding on this representation, MIRA-AC jointly trains one model to forecast remaining return from a curated research observation and choose how to allocate research effort next. Decision-level value forecasting outperforms token-level alternatives, and decision-level policy learning improves gold-evaluated research outcomes, including when trained from proxy feedback, without directly optimizing the longer executor trajectories. We also find initial evidence that actor updates learned across research environments can transfer to a held-out actor-training environment when initialized from a cross-environment value model.\nThe broader implication is that research strategy need not remain an implicit byproduct of long-horizon generation. Once decisions about what to investigate are represented explicitly and linked to their downstream consequences, they become objects that can be valued, credited, and improved through experience. This suggests a path toward research agents that learn not only to execute individual investigations more effectively, but to improve the strategy by which they decide what research is worth doing next.\nReferences\n- \nAutoDiscovery: Open-ended Scientific Discovery via Bayesian Surprise.\nIn Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems,\nNote: arXiv:2507.00310\nExternal Links: [Link](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.00310), [Document](https://dx.doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2507.00310) Cited by: [Appendix A](#A1.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1).\n- \nOptNet: differentiable optimization as a layer in neural networks.\nExternal Links: 1703.00443,\n[Link](https://arxiv.org/abs/1703.00443) Cited by: [item 34](#A2.I1.i34.p1.1).\n- \nTabNet: attentive interpretable tabular learning.\nExternal Links: 1908.07442,\n[Link](https://arxiv.org/abs/1908.07442) Cited by: [item 47](#A2.I1.i47.p1.1).\n- \nZoology: measuring and improving recall in efficient language models.\nExternal Links: 2312.04927,\n[Link](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.04927) Cited by: [item 50](#A2.I1.i50.p1.1).\n- \nInteraction networks for learning about objects, relations and physics.\nExternal Links: 1612.00222,\n[Link](https://arxiv.org/abs/1612.00222) Cited by: [item 19](#A2.I1.i19.p1.1).\n- \nA distributional perspective on reinforcement learning.\nIn Proceedings of the 34th International Conference on Machine Learning,\nVol. 70, pp. 449–458.\nExternal Links: [Link](https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.06887) Cited by: [§1](#S1.p5.1).\n- \nSciMaster: Towards General-Purpose Scientific AI Agents, Part I. Strategies from the Perspective of Search Dynamics.\n arXiv.\nNote: arXiv:2605.17373\nExternal Links: [Link](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17373), [Document](https://dx.doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2605.17373) Cited by: [Appendix A](#A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p2.1), [Appendix A](#A1.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1.1), [§1](#S1.p1.1).\nAppendix Contents\nBackground\n[A  Appendix A Related Work](#A1).[A](#A1)\nExperimental setup\n[B  Appendix B Experimental Setup](#A2).[B](#A2)\n[C  Appendix C MIRA Implementation](#A3).[C](#A3)\nAdditional MIRA analyses\n[D  Appendix D Semantic Exploration Graphs and Strategic Inertia](#A4).[D](#A4)\n[E  Appendix E RMT Architecture Identified by MIRA](#A5).[E](#A5)\n[F  Appendix F Loop Transformer Search and Selected Designs](#A6).[F](#A6)\nCritic and policy-learning details\n[G  Appendix G Critic Training and Validation](#A7).[G](#A7)\n[H  Appendix H MIRA-AC Training Details](#A8).[H](#A8)\n[I  Appendix I Trajectory Stitching Across Artificial
