Boundaries](#A9).[I](#A9)\n[J  Appendix J Additional MIRA-AC Results](#A10).[J](#A10)\n[K  Appendix K Behavioral Analysis of MIRA-AC](#A11).[K](#A11)\nAppendix A Related Work\nRational meta-reasoning.\nMIRA builds on the classical account in Principles of\nMetareasoning ([Russell and Wefald, 1991](#bib.bib57)), which asks which computation a\nresource-bounded agent should perform next and when it should stop. In long-horizon research, the\nrelevant computations are investigations: testing a hypothesis, revising an\nimplementation, gathering evidence, or terminating the search. MIRA makes\nthese allocation decisions explicit as work orders, thereby turning a\nclassical meta-reasoning problem into a trainable sequential decision process.\nBenchmarks for autonomous research.\nRecent benchmarks expose complementary aspects of long-horizon research.\nMLAgentBench ([Huang et al., 2024](#bib.bib7)) and\nMLE-bench ([Chan et al., 2025](#bib.bib6)) evaluate iterative machine-learning\nengineering over executable tasks, while\nRE-Bench ([Wijk et al., 2025](#bib.bib12)) compares agents with human experts over\nincreasing time budgets. PaperBench ([Starace et al., 2025](#bib.bib10)) evaluates\nend-to-end replication using author-developed hierarchical rubrics, and\nHCAST ([Rein et al., 2025](#bib.bib13)) grounds task difficulty in the time required by\nskilled humans. ScienceAgentBench ([Chen et al., 2025](#bib.bib9)) similarly\nderives data-driven discovery tasks from published scientific workflows.\nOther environments broaden either the training interface or the space of\nresearch problems. MLE-Dojo ([Qiang et al., 2025](#bib.bib8)) provides interactive\nKaggle environments with normalized rewards suitable for collecting learning\ntrajectories; PostTrainBench ([Rank et al., 2026](#bib.bib16)) extends\nautoresearch to coupled post-training decisions; and\nFrontierScience ([Wang et al., 2026b](#bib.bib11)) targets expert-level\nscientific tasks. DiscoGen ([Goldie et al., 2026](#bib.bib26)) supplies procedurally\ngenerated algorithm-discovery tasks with meta-train/meta-test splits, whereas\nAlgoTune ([Press et al., 2025](#bib.bib14)) evaluates measurable speedups to established\nnumerical programs. Finally, Frontier-Eng ([Chi et al., 2026](#bib.bib17))\ncharacterizes how improvements diminish over extended engineering runs, and\nFML-bench ([Zou et al., 2026](#bib.bib27)) compares search strategies across\ncontrolled landscapes. In addition to introducing HillClimbBench, we cast\nseveral existing environments as instances of a common research decision\nprocess and ask whether research strategy can be learned across trajectories.\nScientific and algorithmic discovery agents.\nSeveral systems automate substantial portions of the research lifecycle. The\nAI Scientist ([Lu et al., 2024](#bib.bib41)) and\nAI Scientist-v2 ([Yamada et al., 2025](#bib.bib39)) combine experiment execution,\nevaluation, and paper generation, with the latter adding tree search and\nstructured memory. FunSearch ([Romera-Paredes et al., 2024](#bib.bib1)) established\nlanguage-model-guided evolution as a mechanism for discovering programs, and\nAlphaEvolve ([Novikov et al., 2025](#bib.bib35)) scales this approach through model\nensembles, evaluation cascades, and quality-diversity search.\nSAGA ([Du et al., 2025](#bib.bib44)) extends automation to the evolution of\nscientific objectives, while the AI co-scientist ([Gottweis et al., 2026](#bib.bib49))\nand AutoDiscovery ([Agarwal et al., 2025](#bib.bib53)) organize hypothesis\ndevelopment through evolutionary selection and Bayesian surprise,\nrespectively.\nResearch agents also differ in how they divide and retain work.\nR&D-Agent ([Yang et al., 2025](#bib.bib37)) separates researcher and developer roles,\nAutoScientists ([Gao et al., 2026](#bib.bib48)) coordinates decentralized teams\nthrough shared experimental memory, and MARS ([Chen et al., 2026](#bib.bib32)) performs\nbudget-aware tree search over repository states.\nAutoSOTA ([Li et al., 2026](#bib.bib47)) targets reproduction and improvement of\nexisting models, LLM-AutoSciLab ([Kabra et al., 2026](#bib.bib45)) casts\ndiscovery as active experimental design, and Arbor ([Jin et al., 2026](#bib.bib52))\nrefines explicit hypothesis trees.\nDiscoverPhysics ([Wiemann et al., 2026](#bib.bib43)) evaluates open-ended\nscientific reasoning in physics, whereas AInstein ([Mishra et al., 2025](#bib.bib50))\nstudies iterative critique using parametric knowledge alone. These systems\nprimarily improve a fixed model through inference-time organization. MIRA is\ncompatible with such execution mechanisms, but our central concern is learning\nthe policy that chooses the next research objective from prior experience.\nSearch, memory, and evolving agent structure.\nExisting agents encode a wide range of search biases.\nAIDE ([Jiang et al., 2025b](#bib.bib38)) uses explicit drafting, improvement, and debugging\noperators; LATS ([Zhou et al., 2024](#bib.bib25)) combines tree search with acting,\nplanning, reflection, and model-generated values; and\nLoongFlow ([Wan et al., 2025a](#bib.bib33)) couples plan–execute–summarize cycles\nwith evolutionary archives. DataMaster ([Du et al., 2026](#bib.bib31)) schedules\nexploration over the available budget, AIRA ([Pepe et al., 2026](#bib.bib42)) applies\nUCT-style search to neural-architecture discovery, and\nSciMaster ([Chai et al., 2025](#bib.bib34)) distributes exploration across multiple\nagents. Automated AI-research Speedrunning ([Zhao et al., 2025](#bib.bib15)) provides\na controlled autoresearch setting, while Agent\nLaboratory ([Schmidgall et al., 2025](#bib.bib40)) organizes research into fixed phases. The\ndiversity of successful scaffolds, together with the strategy-dependent results\nof Frontier-Eng ([Chi et al., 2026](#bib.bib17)) and\nFML-bench ([Zou et al., 2026](#bib.bib27)), motivates selecting research actions\nconditionally rather than committing to one search heuristic for an entire\ninvestigation.\nA complementary line of work changes the agent’s persistent artifacts.\nSkillGrad ([Wang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib20)) treats trajectory diagnoses as textual\ngradients, and SkillOpt ([Yang et al., 2026](#bib.bib21)) uses bounded edits and\nvalidation to improve reusable skill documents. Harness self-evolution\nanalysis ([Lin et al., 2026](#bib.bib19)) distinguishes the ability to modify a harness\nfrom the ability to benefit from those modifications, while Compiling Agentic\nWorkflows into LLM Weights ([Dennis et al., 2026](#bib.bib18)) shows that procedural\nworkflows can be distilled into model weights. MIRA keeps the execution harness fixed in our\nlearning experiments and instead updates the model that allocates research\neffort; evolving the harness and learning the meta-reasoning policy are\ntherefore complementary directions.\nFeedback and value learning.\nLanguage feedback can convey substantially more structure than a scalar\nreward, as illustrated empirically by LLF-Bench ([Cheng et al., 2023](#bib.bib22))\nand formalized by Formalizing Learning from Language Feedback with Provable\nGuarantees ([Xu et al., 2026](#bib.bib23)). Training AI Co-Scientists Using Rubric\nRewards ([Goel et al., 2025](#bib.bib51)) uses rubric rewards to train research-plan\ngeneration, while HypoAgents ([Duan et al., 2025](#bib.bib54)) uses Bayesian\nbelief and entropy updates to guide which hypotheses to refine. Our experiments instead use\nscalar, outcome-grounded targets: either a gold-binary outcome or changes in\na proxy experimental metric. This choice isolates whether delayed research\noutcomes alone can train value estimates at MIRA’s decision boundaries;\nversioned artifacts could support richer feedback in future work.\nMIRA-AC also relates to shared actor–critic representation learning.\nA3C ([Mnih et al., 2016](#bib.bib58)) uses a shared backbone with separate policy and\nscalar-value outputs, while Phasic Policy Gradient ([Cobbe et al., 2021](#bib.bib59))\nstudies the balance between useful representation transfer and interference\nbetween policy and value objectives. UNREAL ([Jaderberg et al., 2017](#bib.bib60))\ndemonstrates how auxiliary prediction objectives can shape a shared policy\nrepresentation. More recent generative approaches express verification or\nvalue estimation through language modeling itself: Generative\nVerifiers ([Zhang et al., 2025](#bib.bib61)) formulates verification as\nnext-token prediction, and GenAC ([Shan et al., 2026](#bib.bib62)) uses a generative\ncritic for value modeling. MIRA-AC similarly uses\none language model and one next-token interface for both research actions and\nreturn prediction, without adding a scalar head or maintaining a separately\nresident critic.\nCredit assignment in multi-turn agents.\nSeveral methods localize outcome supervision within long interactions. Credit\nassignment can also proceed through a learned step-level critic: Iterative\nGRPO ([Jiang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib4)) fits a turn-level \\(Q^{\\pi}\\) from logged Monte Carlo returns and\nuses it as the reward model for single-turn policy improvement in batch-online\nmulti-turn settings.\nCredit Assignment with Resets ([Samanta et al., 2026a](#bib.bib56)) uses counterfactual\ncontinuations from intermediate states, and Tree-GRPO ([Ji et al., 2026](#bib.bib24))\nobtains step-level relative signals from sibling branches that share a prefix.\nR1-Searcher ([Song et al., 2025](#bib.bib28)) learns retrieval behavior from\noutcome-only reinforcement learning, StepSearch ([Wang et al., 2025](#bib.bib29))\napplies step-wise policy optimization to search-augmented reasoning, and\nstrategy-guided exploration ([Szot et al., 2026](#bib.bib30)) operates over\nhigh-level language strategies. PokeeResearch ([Wan et al., 2025b](#bib.bib46))\nstudies reinforcement-learning design choices for deep-research agents.\nMIRA-AC differs in where it places the\nlearning boundary: it reconstructs complete research episodes but applies\npolicy loss only to the complete sampled decision responses that allocate future\ncomputation. Its decision-level generative critic assigns delayed credit at those\nboundaries, and buffered asynchronous optimization accommodates investigations\nwith heterogeneous durations.\nAppendix B Experimental Setup\nWe study four autoresearch environments: BNLearn causal discovery, Physics Discovery, SRBench symbolic regression, and HillClimbBench-CPU architecture research. In the reported policy evaluation, BNLearn, Physics, and SRBench each contain 100 tasks with one rollout per task. HillClimbBench-CPU contains 50 distinct paper-derived tasks with two independently initialized rollouts per task. Each rollout permits at most 20 MIRA iterations.\nB.1 Shared Interaction Protocol\nEach task is an isolated, persistent workspace. At every iteration, a fresh\nexecutor receives the MIRA work order, the fixed task prompt, and stable\nenvironment-specific instructions. The prompt identifies the available data\nand tools, interaction budget, editable files, submission artifact, and hidden\ninformation. The executor may write analyses and programs, run proxy\nevaluators, update its artifact, and leave evidence for later iterations. The\nshared procedure governing outer-loop and executor interactions is described in\nApp. [C](#A3); below we specify the environment-facing\npart of the prompt.\nFor the three scientific environments, policy training and reported evaluation use independently initialized trajectories on the same fixed task identities and seeds. They therefore test new research trajectories and held-out evaluations within the same tasks, not generalization to unseen task identities.\nB.2 Defining Proxy Hill-Climbing Signals\nEach environment exposes a different proxy measure of progress: graph-edit distance, trajectory error, expression error, or architecture performance. We map that measure to a higher-is-better quality \\(q_{t}\\in[0,1]\\) after iteration \\(t\\), with \\(q_{0}\\) denoting the fixed initial quality. We construct each map from constants in the environment specification and freeze it before training.\nThe four maps use natural task-level anchors. BNLearn measures how much of the empty graph’s structural Hamming distance (SHD) has been removed. Physics scales trajectory mean squared error (MSE) by position variance and applies a fixed reciprocal map capped at \\(0.95\\). SRBench measures log-normalized mean squared error (log-NMSE) improvement from the initial expression toward the float64 numerical floor. HillClimbBench-CPU measures improvement from the supplied reference architecture toward the task’s ideal score of one. The exact equations appear below. The endpoints need not be attainable; they are placed to distinguish plausible within-budget improvements.\nThese proxies provide a task-local ordering of partial progress. They turn changes across successive research iterations into marginal rewards at a resolution suited to the current model class and fixed research budget. The mapping remains imperfect: it can miss real progress, reward a brittle shortcut, or saturate too early. Gold evaluation therefore remains the final measure of the selected artifact. It stays hidden during research, while reward depends on increases in best-so-far proxy quality.\nFor a new environment, the construction begins with a repeatable proxy metric that usually moves with the desired outcome. Natural anchors include a supplied baseline, an empty prediction, or a task-defined target. A monotone transform can spread raw errors that span orders of magnitude, while its scale should keep plausible improvements away from a narrow or saturated range. Invalid artifacts receive no improvement. The completed map is frozen before policy training. Development tasks then establish its usable range and its association with gold outcomes; poor alignment calls for redesign before final evaluation.\nThe \\(q_{t}\\) values define best-so-far progress and marginal reward in\nSec. [2.2](#S2.SS2); other visible diagnostics do not affect reward. After\nselection, a hidden gold evaluation produces the score reported in the results\ntable. It uses the same hidden target as the proxy for BNLearn and separate\nheld-out data or conditions for the other environments. A task is solved when\nthis gold evaluation meets the environment-specific success criterion.\nB.3 Environment Interfaces\nBNLearn causal discovery.\nWe construct 100 tasks from the Alarm (32 tasks), Child (17), Insurance (24),\nand Water (27) networks in the Bayesian Network\nRepository ([Scutari, n.d.](#bib.bib2)). Each task is a randomly\npermuted, anonymized 14–34-node induced subgraph. A linear-Gaussian structural\nequation model provides 1,800 training and 600 visible validation observations.\nThe agent may analyze the visible data, query validation Bayesian information criterion (BIC) diagnostics, and, on an \\(n\\)-node task, request \\(n\\) interventions that report estimated total effects. These diagnostics do not determine reward, and the executor cannot query graph accuracy while carrying out an investigation. The final artifact is a directed acyclic graph (DAG).\nAfter each completed iteration, the harness reports directed structural Hamming distance (SHD), including missing, extra, and reversed edges. For the empty-graph SHD \\(S_{0}\\) and submitted-graph SHD \\(S_{t}\\), proxy quality is \\(q_{t}=\\operatorname{clip}((S_{0}-S_{t})/S_{0},0,1)\\). The best model-authored graph is retained and restored for final scoring under the same hidden-DAG objective. Its gold score is the normalized SHD gap closure above, evaluated on the submitted graph; the task is solved only by exact directed recovery.\nPhysics discovery.\nWe adapt DiscoverPhysics ([Wiemann et al., 2026](#bib.bib43)) into 100 anonymous\nworlds spanning 12 mechanisms and three difficulty levels. The agent receives a\nmission and topology-specific laboratory interface, but not the governing law.\nAcross 20 MIRA iterations, it may allocate 15 experiment rounds among the first\n19; the last iteration is reserved for finalization. It may validate experiment\nplans and repeatedly fit executable candidate laws to all acquired trajectories.\nThe agent selects an executable law and a short explanation. For acquired-trajectory MSE \\(m_{t}\\) and position variance \\(v_{t}\\), proxy quality is \\(q_{t}=0.95/(1+\\sqrt{m_{t}/v_{t}})\\). After the fixed horizon, the law is evaluated once on held-out trajectories and the explanation is graded once by a hosted GPT-5.5 judge using a hidden world-specific reference and rubric. Let \\(n\\) be trajectory MSE normalized by the held-out world’s ground-truth trajectory variance, and let \\(e\\) be the explanation score. For an unsolved task, the gold score is the product of the threshold-scaled components \\(0.1/\\max(n,0.1)\\) and \\(\\min(1,e/0.75)\\), capped strictly below one. A task is solved, and receives a gold score of one, when \\(n<0.1\\) and \\(e\\geq 0.75\\).\nSRBench symbolic regression.\nWe use 100 LLM-SRBench problems ([Shojaee et al., 2025](#bib.bib3)) from chemical\nreactions (30 tasks), physical oscillators (27), materials science (23), and\npopulation growth (20). Within each family, tasks span a frozen\nreference-expression complexity ranking. The official training data are split\ndeterministically 80/20 into visible fit and validation sets; the\nin-distribution (ID) test set, optional out-of-distribution (OOD) set, and\nreference expression remain hidden.\nThe agent may analyze the available data freely and evaluate expressions within a constrained SymPy-compatible grammar. It must select one final expression. The environment reports proxy metrics but does not restore the candidate with the highest proxy metric, so final selection remains with the agent.\nFor validation NMSE \\(N_{t}\\), fixed initial-expression NMSE \\(B\\), and float64 floor \\(\\epsilon\\), proxy quality is\n|  | \\[q_{t}=\\operatorname{clip}\\!\\left(\\frac{\\log(B/\\max\\{N_{t},\\epsilon\\})}{\\log(B/\\epsilon)},0,1\\right).\\] |  | (5) | \nThe final expression is evaluated once on sealed ID-test data, with OOD metrics reported when available. Its gold score is \\(R^{2}\\) on the sealed ID test. The task is solved when pointwise relative error is at most \\(0.1\\) everywhere (and the prediction is exact when the target is zero).\nHillClimbBench-CPU architecture research.\nWe select 50 research papers whose central architectural contribution can be implemented, trained, evaluated, and iteratively improved within a CPU-only budget, and construct one task from each paper. We run two independent trajectories per task. Each task prompt combines a technical paper summary, research brief, description of how gold evaluation differs from proxy evaluation, evaluation contract, and reference implementation. The agent tests architectural hypotheses while the host fixes the data, targets, loss, optimizer, training budget, evaluation seeds, and resource limits. Every candidate is trained from scratch on one CPU thread.\nThe 50 source papers are:\n- 1. \nCan Recurrent Neural Networks Warp Time? ([Tallec and Ollivier, 2018](#bib.bib75));\n- 2. \nAn Intriguing Failing of Convolutional Neural Networks and the CoordConv Solution ([Liu et al., 2018](#bib.bib76));\n- 3. \nDeep Lattice Networks and Partial Monotonic Functions ([You et al., 2017](#bib.bib77));\n- 4. \nDeep Sets ([Zaheer et al., 2017](#bib.bib78));\n- 5. \nLearning Nonlinear Operators via DeepONet Based on the Universal Approximation Theorem of Operators ([Lu et al., 2021](#bib.bib79));\n- 6. \nDynamical Isometry and a Mean Field Theory of RNNs: Gating Enables Signal Propagation in Recurrent Neural Networks ([Chen et al., 2018a](#bib.bib80));\n- 7. \nE(n) Equivariant Graph Neural Networks ([Satorras et al., 2021](#bib.bib81));\n- 8. \nFourier Features Let Networks Learn High Frequency Functions in Low Dimensional Domains ([Tancik et al., 2020](#bib.bib82));\n- 9. \nFull-Capacity Unitary Recurrent Neural Networks ([Wisdom et al., 2016](#bib.bib83));\n- 10. \nHow Powerful are Graph Neural Networks? ([Xu et al., 2018](#bib.bib84));\n- 11. \nGraph Attention Networks ([Veličković et al., 2017](#bib.bib85));\n- 12. \nGrid Long Short-Term Memory ([Kalchbrenner et al., 2015](#bib.bib86));\n- 13. \nGroup Equivariant Convolutional Networks ([Cohen and Welling, 2016](#bib.bib87));\n- 14. \nLearning Latent Permutations with Gumbel-Sinkhorn Networks ([Mena et al., 2018](#bib.bib88));\n- 15. \nHiPPO: Recurrent Memory with Optimal Polynomial Projections ([Gu et al., 2020](#bib.bib89));\n- 16. \nHyena Hierarchy: Towards Larger Convolutional Language Models ([Poli et al., 2023](#bib.bib90));\n- 17. \nHyperNetworks ([Ha et al., 2016](#bib.bib91));\n- 18. \nIndependently Recurrent Neural Network (IndRNN): Building A Longer and Deeper RNN ([Li et al., 2018](#bib.bib92));\n- 19. \nInteraction Networks for Learning about Objects, Relations and Physics ([Battaglia et al., 2016](#bib.bib93));\n- 20. \nThe Unreasonable Effectiveness of the Forget Gate ([van der Westhuizen and Lasenby, 2018](#bib.bib94));\n- 21. \nJanossy Pooling: Learning Deep Permutation-Invariant Functions for Variable-Size Inputs ([Murphy et al., 2018](#bib.bib95));\n- 22. \nKAN: Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks ([Liu et al., 2024](#bib.bib96));\n- 23. \nLegendre Memory Units: Continuous-Time Representation in Recurrent Neural Networks ([Voelker et al., 2019](#bib.bib97));\n- 24. \nResurrecting Recurrent Neural Networks for Long Sequences ([Orvieto et al., 2023](#bib.bib98));\n- 25. \nLong Short-Term Memory ([Hochreiter and Schmidhuber, 1997](#bib.bib99));\n- 26. \nMamba: Linear-Time Sequence Modeling with Selective State Spaces ([Gu and Dao, 2023](#bib.bib100));\n- 27. \nMemory Networks ([Weston et al., 2014](#bib.bib101));\n- 28. \nMinimalRNN: Toward More Interpretable and Trainable Recurrent Neural Networks ([Chen, 2017](#bib.bib102));\n- 29. \nHopfield Networks is All You Need ([Ramsauer et al., 2020](#bib.bib103));\n- 30. \nNeural Arithmetic Units ([Madsen and Johansen, 2020](#bib.bib104));\n- 31. \nNeural Ordinary Differential Equations ([Chen et al., 2018b](#bib.bib105));\n- 32. \nNeural Processes ([Garnelo et al., 2018](#bib.bib106));\n- 33. \nNeural Turing Machines ([Graves et al., 2014](#bib.bib107));\n- 34. \nOptNet: Differentiable Optimization as a Layer in Neural Networks ([Amos and Kolter, 2017](#bib.bib108));\n- 35. \nParallelizing Legendre Memory Unit Training ([Chilkuri and Eliasmith, 2021](#bib.bib109));\n- 36. \nPDE-Net: Learning PDEs from Data ([Long et al., 2017](#bib.bib110));\n- 37. \nPerceiver IO: A General Architecture for Structured Inputs & Outputs ([Jaegle et al., 2021](#bib.bib111));\n- 38. \nRethinking Attention with Performers ([Choromanski et al., 2020](#bib.bib112));\n- 39. \nPhased LSTM: Accelerating Recurrent Network Training for Long or Event-based Sequences ([Neil et al., 2016](#bib.bib113));\n- 40. \nPointer Networks ([Vinyals et al., 2015](#bib.bib114));\n- 41. \nRecurrent Highway Networks ([Zilly et al., 2016](#bib.bib115));\n- 42. \nEfficiently Modeling Long Sequences with Structured State Spaces ([Gu et al., 2021](#bib.bib116));\n- 43. \nOrthogonal Recurrent Neural Networks with Scaled Cayley Transform ([Helfrich et al., 2017](#bib.bib117));\n- 44. \nImplicit Neural Representations with Periodic Activation Functions ([Sitzmann et al., 2020](#bib.bib118));\n- 45. \nObject-Centric Learning with Slot Attention ([Locatello et al., 2020](#bib.bib119));\n- 46. \nSpatial Transformer Networks ([Jaderberg et al., 2015](#bib.bib120));\n- 47. \nTabNet: Attentive Interpretable Tabular Learning ([Arik and Pfister, 2019](#bib.bib121));\n- 48. \nLess is More: Recursive Reasoning with Tiny Networks ([Jolicoeur-Martineau, 2025](#bib.bib122));\n- 49. \nUniversal Transformers ([Dehghani et al., 2018](#bib.bib123)); and\n- 50. \nZoology: Measuring and Improving Recall in Efficient Language Models ([Arora et al., 2023](#bib.bib124)).\nThe proxy evaluator measures both in-distribution performance and a task-specific structural extrapolation split. Each task fixes in advance a scoring function that combines these measurements into a scalar proxy metric \\(u_{t}\\in[0,1]\\), with one denoting the task’s declared ideal. Only valid candidates whose proxy metric exceeds that of the retained incumbent replace it. For the frozen reference metric \\(u_{0}\\), proxy quality is \\(q_{t}=\\operatorname{clip}((u_{t}-u_{0})/(1-u_{0}),0,1)\\). After selection, the retained architecture and task reference are retrained on the same five gold-evaluation seeds under harder structural conditions. The task’s gold evaluator maps each run to a score in \\([0,1]\\), and the submission’s gold score is the mean across its five runs. A submission meets the benchmark’s success criterion when the lower endpoint of the two-sided 95% Student-\\(t\\) confidence interval over the five paired candidate-minus-reference scores is positive. In the policy comparison, a win is a paired evaluation in which the trained-policy submission has a higher gold score than the matched untrained-MIRA submission.\nB.4 Evaluation\nWe report the proxy best-so-far quality \\(\\bar{q}_{t}\\) and the gold score separately. Comparisons use matched task instances and interaction budgets; confidence intervals use a task-clustered bootstrap.\nAppendix C MIRA Implementation\nThis appendix describes how MIRA constructs each decision context, executes the resulting work order, and delegates evaluation and submission to the task environment.\nC.1 Context Construction\nThe outer-loop context is rebuilt at every iteration. Its fixed portion contains the task statement, a compact workspace summary, the latest proxy evaluation, a structured summary of the immediately preceding work episode and a pointer to its handover when available, and the remaining budget. The full episode record and handover text are not copied automatically. Instead, the fixed curator has read-only access to repository files, diffs, commit history, and prior work records, from which it selects a bounded set of decision-relevant evidence. The fixed portion and this selected evidence form the new decision context.\nThe policy is instructed to select a bounded objective or coherent experiment set while considering its information value, cost, remaining time, and risk to the current solution. Its work order states the objective, relevant evidence, resource guidance, acceptance criteria, and stopping condition. The same structure is used for theorem proving, with experiments replaced by proof construction and verification.\nC.2 Decision and Execution\nA fresh executor in the Codex harness receives the work order, its selected repository references, and the stable environment instructions. It may inspect and modify the workspace, run tests and authorized tools, and evaluate candidates. It validates substantive work, commits useful artifacts and evidence, and leaves a concise handover. Recorded failures and contradictory results remain available to later decisions through the repository.\nThe repository—including the current candidate, experiments, results, analyses, and handovers—persists across iterations; the model contexts do not. Any proxy evaluation produced after execution becomes evidence for the next decision. When an environment maintains an incumbent, it protects the strongest validated artifact against later regressions.\nC.3 Environment-Controlled Evaluation and Submission\nThe environment adapter supplies the task-specific prompt, tools, proxy feedback, reward mapping, submission artifact, and terminal evaluator. It also determines who authorizes submission: Codex may submit under inner-loop control, the policy chooses between further investigation and submission under outer-loop control, and the environment evaluates only after research ends under external control.\nA run ends when a model-visible success condition is met, submission is authorized, or the iteration or wall-clock budget is exhausted. Gold evaluation remains outside both model contexts and is applied only to the selected repository artifact. Environments that retain an incumbent may restore it before final evaluation.\nAppendix D Semantic Exploration Graphs and Strategic Inertia\nWe construct the exploration graphs after each run has terminated. The graphs are used only for analysis and are never shown to the evaluated system.\nComparable evidence projection.\nWe project every run into the same chronological evidence format: the common task statement, text authored by the evaluated models, and the submitted proof. For MIRA, model-authored text includes the outer-loop policy’s work orders and the executors’ reports; for Codex, it includes assistant messages. We exclude commands, tool outputs, diffs, commits, repository metadata, handovers, submission reviews, and external evaluations from every condition. The same projection is applied at each forced-continuation checkpoint, and the same downstream extraction procedure is applied to every inference system.\nSemantic graph extraction.\nA GPT-5.5 pass at temperature zero converts the projected trace into a graph. A node represents a semantically meaningful proof or research step, such as an approach, claim, lemma, counterexample, experiment, candidate, result, or submission. Several raw turns may therefore map to one node, while one long response may contain several nodes. Nodes record their status, such as supported, proved, refuted, stalled, abandoned, or submitted. Typed edges record evidence-supported relationships including refinement, dependence, support, refutation, pivoting, supersession, and production of a result. Chronology alone does not create an edge.\nThe extractor receives the trajectory content but not the method name, model name, grader feedback, or final grader score. Every node and edge must cite a source segment in the projected evidence; outputs without valid provenance are rejected. The extracted nodes are ordered by first appearance in the trace. For visualization, the submission ancestry is placed on a central lane and other connected components are assigned separate lanes. Shortened labels and display coordinates affect only presentation, not the analysis.\nResearch-direction annotation.\nA separate GPT-5.5 pass at temperature zero groups the fixed semantic nodes into research directions. A direction is a high-level hypothesis, proof plan, construction, mechanism, or algorithmic approach. Lemmas, tests, repairs, and refinements remain in the same direction when they serve the same high-level plan; a materially different plan receives a new identifier, and a later return to a previous plan reuses its identifier. This pass may correct the order of fixed nodes when their provenance establishes a different first-introduction order, but it may not add, remove, split, or merge nodes. It observes node content, provenance episode identifiers, and graph relations, but not the method or model name, final grader score, grader feedback, node status, terminal submission, graph title, or graph summary.\nStrategic inertia.\nTwo consecutive non-submission semantic steps form a continuation when they belong to the same annotated research direction and a pivot otherwise. For graph \\(\\mathcal{G}\\), we define \\(I(\\mathcal{G})=N_{\\mathrm{continue}}/(N_{\\mathrm{continue}}+N_{\\mathrm{pivot}})\\). The root and terminal submission are excluded. Graphs with fewer than two non-submission nodes have no transitions and receive no inertia value. The statistic is descriptive: high inertia can sustain a promising direction or prolong a local minimum, while low inertia can expose alternatives or reflect premature switching.\nFor the natural GPT-5.5 comparison, the analysis contains 30 problems, three trials, and three inference systems. We compute inertia for each run, average trials within each problem, and obtain 95% confidence intervals by resampling problems with 10,000 bootstrap draws. Pairwise contrasts match runs by problem and trial, average the paired differences within each problem, and again bootstrap problems. Natural and forced-continuation runs are analyzed separately.\nThe four visualizations below are the problems on which a gap remains after output-token-matched continuation. They show the first natural-stopping trial and use the same extraction and layout procedure for all three inference systems.\nAppendix E RMT Architecture Identified by MIRA\nMatched search protocol.\nWe run one 24-hour GPT-5.5 campaign for each orchestration condition. All three campaigns use the same task reference architecture, proxy evaluator, compute budget, and parameter limit. Proxy evaluations guide research, and the final submitted architecture is scored separately by the gold evaluator.\nSelected architecture.\nAn ordinary RMT stores each token’s residual state as a matrix \\(X_{\\ell}\\in\\mathbb{R}^{D_{k}\\times D_{v}}\\). Attention and MLP branches read this state through a lower-rank interface, produce a matrix update \\(\\Delta X_{\\ell}^{(b)}\\), and add it uniformly as \\(X_{\\ell,\\mathrm{out}}^{(b)}=X_{\\ell,\\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}+\\Delta X_{\\ell}^{(b)}\\) for \\(b\\in\\{\\mathrm{attn},\\mathrm{mlp}\\}\\). MIRA’s final model retains this matrix state and low-rank read/write interface, uses a GELU-gated linear unit (GEGLU) feed-forward block, and introduces a separate learned gate for each branch and layer: \\(X_{\\ell,\\mathrm{out}}^{(b)}=X_{\\ell,\\mathrm{in}}^{(b)}+G_{\\ell}^{(b)}\\odot\\Delta X_{\\ell}^{(b)}\\), where \\(G_{\\ell}^{(b)}\\in\\mathbb{R}^{D_{k}\\times D_{v}}\\). Every coefficient is initialized to one, so the new update is exactly the ordinary additive update at initialization. The two gates add only 0.056% to the model’s parameter count. Unlike a generic GEGLU block, this mechanism is specific to the matrix residual stream: it learns where each branch can amplify or suppress a proposed matrix-state write.\nMIRA first tested the gate at small scale, then reran a final-scale comparison\nmatched in geometry, seed, optimizer, and training tokens. Relative to the\nungated GEGLU control, the gated model improved last-token cross-entropy by\n0.009 at small scale and by 0.021 at the final scale.\nBoth comparisons use one seed, and the ungated final-geometry control was not\nsubmitted to the gold evaluator. These ablations support the gate\nas a promising mechanism, but they do not isolate it as the cause of the full\ngold-loss improvement in Fig. [4](#S6.F4)(b).\nThe learned gates did not remain near their identity initialization. Attention gates generally amplify writes, whereas MLP gates generally suppress them, and both vary with depth. A two-way additive decomposition into row and column effects explains roughly three quarters of the variation in the depth-averaged attention map and nearly nine tenths in the MLP map, with stronger variation along the residual-key axis. This supports a structured, branch-, depth-, and address-dependent write-allocation interpretation. It does not establish dynamic routing: the gates are fixed across tokens and examples, and their low exact-map correlation across layers does not support a clean permanent partition into “durable” and “temporary” cells.\nAppendix F Loop Transformer Search and Selected Designs\nMatched search protocol.\nWe compare matched 48-hour MIRA and Codex campaigns using GPT-6 Astra. Both\ncampaigns use the same model-scale and training-token rungs.\nAt each rung, candidates and the task reference architecture are evaluated on the\nsame 512-context proxy set with eight recurrent iterations. Intermediate\nsearch decisions use these results; after submission, the final incumbent from\neach campaign and the task reference are evaluated on a separate\n4,873-context gold set. The proxy and gold results are reported\nseparately in Fig. [6](#S6.F6).\nMIRA search trajectory.\nMIRA begins by hypothesizing that repeated applications of the shared core\noverwrite useful state. It compares a learned adaptive retention gate with a\nconstant interpolation between the previous and proposed states. The learned\ngate does not outperform this control, so subsequent candidates retain the\nsimpler leaky update. After explicit fast and slow states, iteration-history\nmechanisms, and other routing alternatives produce small or inconsistent gains,\nMIRA hypothesizes that repeated positive attention may reinforce information\nthat later recurrences struggle to remove. It tests the resulting subtractive\nattention mechanism against a matched positive-mixture control, then promotes\nit only after the advantage persists across seeds and model scales.\nThe selected design combines what the campaign calls “signed attention”\nwith a leaky recurrent update (Fig. [16](#A6.F16)). If the two\nbranches produce attention reads \\(A_{1}\\)\nand \\(A_{2}\\) over shared values, the attention module returns\n\\(A_{1}+(A_{1}-A_{2})\\). Each branch separately uses ordinary softmax attention;\nthe subtraction creates effective positive and negative coefficients over the\nshared values. The second branch copies the first branch’s query\nand key parameters at initialization, making the additional contrastive term\nexactly zero before training. Training can then break this symmetry, allowing\nthe effective coefficients to suppress as well as reinforce value components.\nAfter the block proposes \\(F(h^{(k)})\\) from recurrent state \\(h^{(k)}\\), the\nleaky update mixes the previous and proposed states,\n\\(h^{(k+1)}=\\rho h^{(k)}+(1-\\rho)F(h^{(k)})\\), with fixed \\(\\rho=0.25\\). The\nattention variant and leaky update therefore play complementary roles: one supports\nsubtractive routing, while the other limits how completely a recurrent step\ncan overwrite its input. Applied in a Loop Transformer, this mechanism\nresembles a fixed-coefficient variant of Differential\nAttention ([Ye et al., 2025](#bib.bib68)). It uses two full query–key branches\ninitialized identically and the fixed mixture \\(2A_{1}-A_{2}\\).\nComparison.\nCodex selects a tangent-state update with local/global attention routing and learned write gates on both the attention and MLP branches, a complementary strategy that regulates how aggressively each recurrent pass changes the state. MIRA’s incumbent improves proxy loss at every completed scale. Its final proxy NLL is 3.0331, compared with 3.2419 for Codex and 3.2940 for the task reference. On the separate gold evaluation, its NLL is 3.0464, compared with 3.2275 for Codex and 3.2653 for the task reference.\nAppendix G Critic Training and Validation\nTargets and value contracts.\nAll critic variants use the stitched Monte Carlo target \\(G_{t}\\). Under\nproxy-marginal supervision, \\(G_{t}\\) is the future increase in best-so-far proxy\nquality, where \\(q_{t}\\) is defined for each environment in\nApp. [B.2](#A2.SS2). The corresponding proxy qualities are\ndirected-SHD gap closure for BNLearn, acquired-trajectory normalized fit for\nPhysics Discovery, validation log-NMSE gap closure for SRBench, and\ninitial-to-ideal proxy-score gap closure for HillClimbBench-CPU.\nUnder gold-binary supervision, the label \\(Y\\in\\{0,1\\}\\) records whether\nthe final submission meets the environment’s gold success criterion. This\nmeans exact directed recovery for BNLearn; the joint hidden-trajectory and\nexplanation criterion for Physics Discovery; the ID-test pointwise-accuracy\ncriterion for SRBench; and a positive lower endpoint of the paired 95%\nconfidence interval for improvement over the reference architecture for\nHillClimbBench-CPU. These are the same success criteria specified in\nApp. [B](#A2); the policy-comparison win rate is not a critic\ntarget.\nCritic parameterizations and objectives.\nFor proxy-marginal targets, a scalar critic minimizes\n\\(\\mathcal{L}_{\\mathrm{scalar}}(\\phi)=M^{-1}\\sum_{m=1}^{M}(V_{\\phi}(o_{m})-G_{m})^{2}\\).\nFor the categorical critics, let \\(z_{1},\\ldots,z_{K}\\) be the ordered support. If\n\\(z_{j}<G_{t}<z_{j+1}\\), we set\n\\(y_{j}(G_{t})=(z_{j+1}-G_{t})/(z_{j+1}-z_{j})\\) and\n\\(y_{j+1}(G_{t})=(G_{t}-z_{j})/(z_{j+1}-z_{j})\\), with all other entries zero; if\n\\(G_{t}=z_{j}\\), then \\(y_{j}(G_{t})=1\\). A categorical\nhead produces \\(q_{\\phi}(z_{i}\\mid o)=\\operatorname{softmax}(h_{\\phi}(o))_{i}\\), while\nthe generative critic uses the restricted next-token distribution\n\\(p_{\\phi}(z_{i}\\mid o)\\) from Sec. [4.1](#S4.SS1). They minimize\n\\(\\mathcal{L}_{\\mathrm{cat}}(\\phi)=M^{-1}\\sum_{m=1}^{M}D_{\\mathrm{KL}}(y(G_{m})\\,\\|\\,p_{\\phi}(\\cdot\\mid o_{m}))\\), replacing \\(p_{\\phi}\\) with\n\\(q_{\\phi}\\) for the categorical head. For the generative critic, the\nimplementation gathers the next-token log probabilities of the support labels,\nrenormalizes them across that support, and evaluates the KL objective in\nSec. [4.2](#S4.SS2); no probability mass outside the support enters\nthe critic loss.\nUnder gold-binary supervision, the scalar critic outputs a single logit \\(\\ell_{\\phi}(o)\\) and represents value as \\(V_{\\phi}(o)=\\sigma(\\ell_{\\phi}(o))\\). It is trained with binary cross-entropy with logits,\n|  | \\[\\mathcal{L}_{\\mathrm{bin}}(\\phi)=-\\frac{1}{M}\\sum_{m=1}^{M}\\left[Y_{m}\\log\\sigma(\\ell_{\\phi}(o_{m}))+(1-Y_{m})\\log\\!\\left(1-\\sigma(\\ell_{\\phi}(o_{m}))\\right)\\right].\\] |  | (6) | \nThe gold-binary generative critic instead restricts the next-token distribution to the support \\(\\{0,1\\}\\) and uses the corresponding one-hot target. Token-level variants repeat the decision return at each causal token prefix within the action; decision-level variants forecast once from the curated observation.\nTarget and forecast validation.\nProxy-marginal returns are validated and clipped to \\(G_{t}\\in[0,1-\\bar{q}_{t-1}]\\). Before bootstrapping, temporal-difference targets, or advantage estimation, proxy-marginal forecasts must be finite and are clipped to the same interval. For a generative critic, \\(V_{\\phi}(o_{t})=\\sum_{i}p_{\\phi}(z_{i}\\mid o_{t})z_{i}\\). Gold-binary targets lie in \\(\\{0,1\\}\\), and both the sigmoid head value and the expected value of the two-point generative distribution lie in \\([0,1]\\).\nPretraining and target-environment adaptation.\nWe pretrain Qwen3.6-27B for one epoch with batch size 8, microbatch size 1,\nmaximum sequence length 262,144, and seed 0. The optimizer is AdamW with\nlearning rate \\(10^{-5}\\), weight decay 0.01, gradient clipping at 1.0, 5%\nlinear warmup, and cosine decay to 10% of the peak learning rate. Training\nuses bfloat16 and the LoRA configuration in\nTable [3](#A8.T3). The\nvalue-forecasting experiments use four H200s; the four-environment critic used\nto initialize the general MIRA-AC policy uses eight H200s.\nFor target-environment adaptation, we evaluate support sizes\n\\(\\{0,1,5,10,25,50\\}\\) over five paired support/query splits with 50 query\nepisodes per split. Adaptation makes one episode-ordered pass using AdamW at\nlearning rate \\(10^{-5}\\) and LoRA dropout 0.05 on one H200.\nFor each target environment and paired support/query split, we first average the single-source pretraining condition across source environments. We then report averages over target environments and splits with 95% confidence intervals from a paired bootstrap over support/query splits.\nGenerative critic prompts.\nThe generative critic receives the same curated decision-scoped conversation\nas the actor, followed by the value query in Fig. [17](#A7.F17).\nWhen forecasting at termination, the query marks the normal research budget as\nexhausted.\nFor gold-binary supervision, the environment-specific contract states the gold success event above, and the proxy-marginal question and eleven-point support are replaced by:\nPredict the binary terminal outcome of the complete research episode.\n0.0 means the environment’s authoritative terminal success condition will not be met.\n1.0 means the environment’s authoritative terminal success condition will be met.\nUse the raw metrics, errors, tests, benchmark scores, experiment results,\ncurrent progress, and remaining budget as evidence.\nWill this research episode ultimately succeed?\nA. 0.0\nB. 1.0\nAnswer with only the single letter for your estimate.\nAppendix H MIRA-AC Training Details\nAll policy-training runs use Qwen3.6-27B ([Qwen Team, 2026](#bib.bib73))\n(Qwen-27B) with LoRA adapters. In separate actor–critic configurations, actor\nadapters target the standard attention and MLP projections,\nmixture-of-experts (MoE) experts, and latent fully connected projections. MIRA-AC targets the\nstandard attention and MLP projections together with the model’s recurrent\ninput and output projections. Separate-critic variants use distinct policy and\ncritic adapters. In MIRA-AC, one shared adapter serves both roles, with separate\noptimizer states for actor and critic updates; online critic updates modify only\nthe shared adapter’s MLP LoRA parameters. Each critic is initialized from a pretrained checkpoint\nmatched to its critic parameterization and target environment.\nSeparate actors start from the untrained MIRA policy. For each single-environment\nexperiment, MIRA-AC initializes its shared adapter from a generative critic\npretrained on trajectories from that environment under the matching supervision\ntarget. The cross-environment policy instead starts from a proxy-marginal\ngenerative critic pretrained jointly across all four environments, then receives\nonline actor–critic updates on SRBench, Physics Discovery, and\nHillClimbBench-CPU.\nTable [3](#A8.T3) reports the configuration of the general\ncross-environment policy used for the main result. The reported checkpoint is\nthe epoch-one shared adapter at version 94, after 49 actor updates and 90 critic\nupdates. When both learners update in one transaction, their updates share a\nsingle published version.\n| Setting | Value | \n|---|---|\n| Base model | Qwen3.6-27B, revision 6a9e13bd6fc8; policy thinking enabled and critic thinking disabled | \n| Initialization | Weights-only initialization from a proxy-marginal decision-level generative critic pretrained jointly across four environments | \n| Actor-training distribution | SRBench, Physics Discovery, and HillClimbBench-CPU; 100 episodes per environment, one epoch, run seed 42 | \n| LoRA | Rank 32, scale 64, dropout 0; shared actor–critic adapter; online critic updates restricted to MLP projections | \n| Research and sequence limits | 20 research decisions; 262,144-token maximum sequence; 8,192-token maximum generation | \n| Policy sampling | Temperature 1.0, top-\\(p\\) 0.95, top-\\(k\\) 20, minimum-\\(p\\) 0, presence penalty 0, repetition penalty 1 | \n| Actor optimizer | AdamW, learning rate \\(10^{-5}\\), \\(\\beta=(0.9,0.999)\\), weight decay 0.01, gradient clipping 1.0, constant schedule | \n| Online critic optimizer | AdamW, learning rate \\(5\\times 10^{-6}\\), \\(\\beta=(0.9,0.999)\\), \\(\\epsilon=10^{-8}\\), weight decay 0.01, gradient clipping 1.0; one pass and optimizer step per sealed batch | \n| Precision | 32-bit floating-point (FP32) actor optimization and reduction; bfloat16 critic backbone with FP32 logits and targets | \n| Return and GAE | Nonnegative marginal improvement in best proxy quality; \\(\\gamma=1\\); \\(\\lambda_{T}=1-1/(\\alpha T)\\) with \\(\\alpha=20\\) | \n| Actor objective | \\(\\epsilon_{\\mathrm{low}}=0.3\\), \\(\\epsilon_{\\mathrm{high}}=5.0\\), \\(\\tau_{A}=1\\), \\(\\tau_{\\mathrm{KL}}=10^{-3}\\); normalized by research decision | \n| Active episodes | Up to 32 asynchronous episodes; completed slots are refilled immediately; new parameters are adopted at decision boundaries | \n| Actor buffer | At least 4 episodes, 64 decisions, and 262,144 trainable tokens; 15-minute fallback after episode and decision minima | \n| Critic buffer | At least 2 episodes, 32 decisions, and 131,072 prompt tokens; 7.5-minute fallback after episode and decision minima | \n| Publication | When ready, critic and actor updates execute in that order within one atomic transaction, followed by one shared-version publication | \n| Compute | 16 H200s for actor–critic inference and optimization, plus 16 H200s serving frozen executors | \nWe train one policy per configuration. Each environment-specific run makes one pass over a fixed ordered set of 100 episodes. The cross-environment run uses 300 episodes: 100 from each of its three actor-training environments. The scientific environments use 100 tasks with one rollout each; HillClimbBench-CPU uses 50 tasks with two rollouts each. Within each environment, the task order is deterministically shuffled from run seed 42; environments are interleaved by equal-weight deficit round robin, and generation seeds are tied to logical decision identifiers.\nThe actor packs each sealed learner batch dynamically into microbatches of at\nmost 262,144 tokens and takes one optimizer step after accumulating their\ngradients. The online critic uses microbatch size 1 across four GPUs and takes\none optimizer step after a complete pass without replacement through each sealed\nbatch. The\nproxy generative critic uses support \\(\\{0.0,0.1,\\ldots,1.0\\}\\); the gold-binary\ncritic forecasts the gold-binary outcome on support \\(\\{0,1\\}\\). We use \\(\\gamma=1\\)\nand exact Monte Carlo critic targets.\nActor advantages use the episode-length-adaptive GAE configuration in\nTable [3](#A8.T3).\nThe implementation uses the token-level importance ratio and range mask from\nSec. [5.3](#S5.SS3), together with the decision-normalized actor\nobjective in Eq. [3](#S5.E3).\nTokens outside the ratio interval\nare excluded from the policy-gradient term but remain in the squared-log-ratio\npenalty. The trainable span contains only the complete sampled decision\nresponse—including its emitted reasoning and end-of-turn token—whether it\nends in a work order or a termination decision. Prompts, curated\nobservations, tool calls, and executor outputs are masked.\nThe buffered schedule maintains at most 32 active episodes and immediately\nrefills completed slots while budget remains. Every completed episode enters\nboth buffers, which seal independently according to\nTable [3](#A8.T3); remaining examples form final underfilled\nbatches. In MIRA-AC, all ready role-specific updates are applied within one\natomic transaction. The critic update precedes the actor update, recorded values remain\ndetached during actor training, and the resulting shared adapter is published\nonce. Live episodes adopt it only at the next meta-reasoning boundary.\nValue-forecasting critic pretraining uses four H200 GPUs. The four-environment critic used to initialize the general policy instead uses eight H200 GPUs. The reported general-policy run uses two eight-H200 nodes for actor–critic inference and optimization and a separate two-node, 16-H200 pool for frozen executors.\nContext-cost accounting.\nThe context-cost analysis in Fig. [11](#S6.F11) compares\nMIRA-AC with fixed-trajectory replays that preserve the recorded work orders,\nexecutor actions, and tool interactions. The MIRA-AC baseline counts policy\ninput together with exact or imputed executor input. The no-compaction replay\ncarries all prior cross-iteration history into each later model call, while the auto-compaction\nprojection caps that history at 200k tokens. The displayed range captures how\noften the carried history is reread. Its lower endpoint adds the history once\nduring each active stage—curation, work-order selection, and execution. Its\nupper endpoint follows a sequential tool loop in which the updated context is\nread after each recorded curation request and executor turn. Frozen-curator\nbase prompts and outputs, critic inputs, compaction-summary costs, and\nbehavioral changes are not modeled. The comparison therefore concerns\nrollout-generation cost, not optimization cost or sample efficiency.\nAppendix I Trajectory Stitching Across Artificial Boundaries\nMIRA reconstructs a fresh curated context at every research decision. Context-window limits, compaction, sequence packing, and learner batching may further split a single logical episode into multiple samples. None of these boundaries is an environment termination. Treating them as terminal either removes future TD residuals from earlier advantages or places later rewards at the wrong temporal distance.\nLet a logical episode \\(\\tau=(\\xi_{1},\\ldots,\\xi_{T})\\) be partitioned into stored samples \\(\\mathcal{S}_{1},\\ldots,\\mathcal{S}_{K}\\). The partition does not alter the termination indicator: \\(d_{t}=1\\) only when the environment truly terminates. Each stored transition contains \\(o_{t}\\), \\(a_{t}\\), \\(r_{t}\\), \\(o_{t+1}\\), actor behavior log probabilities and version \\(\\theta^{b_{t}}\\), and the critic version \\(c_{t}\\) assigned to the transition. For that critic version, the system records both the current baseline \\(v_{t}=V_{\\theta^{c_{t}}}(o_{t})\\) and its successor bootstrap \\(\\widetilde{v}_{t+1}^{(c_{t})}=V_{\\theta^{c_{t}}}(o_{t+1})\\).\nAn asynchronous episode may adopt a different critic before decision \\(t+1\\).\nAt a nonterminal boundary where this occurs, we use the bridge \\(B_{t}\\) from\nSec. [5.2](#S5.SS2), and set \\(B_{t}=0\\) otherwise. Terminal successor\nvalues and bridges are zero. The implemented residual and backward recurrence\nfollow Sec. [5.2](#S5.SS2) and Eq. [2](#S5.E2).\nThe bridge aligns the outgoing critic’s successor baseline with the baseline\nrecorded by the critic version assigned to the next decision.\nAfter this episode-level pass, \\(\\hat{A}_{t}\\) is scattered back to its original sample and token span for actor training. The critic target is the Monte Carlo return \\(G_{t}\\), computed separately over the same reconstructed episode. The recorded value forecasts are treated as constants during the actor update, so the policy loss does not differentiate through the shared critic baseline.\nPartition invariance.\nFor any partition of a fixed logical episode into stored samples, stitching produces the same actor advantages and Monte Carlo critic targets as processing the unpartitioned episode, provided the logged transitions, critic versions, and successor bootstraps are preserved. Stitching restores the same ordered rewards, terminal indicators, baselines, and cross-version bridges. A backward induction on the recurrence therefore yields the same \\(\\hat{A}_{t}\\) at every decision, while every reward remains in the same Monte Carlo return.\nIn our implementation, \\(\\gamma=1\\) and the critic is trained on the exact Monte Carlo return \\(G_{t}=\\sum_{u=t}^{T}r_{u}\\). Actor advantages use episode-length-adaptive \\(\\lambda_{T}=1-1/(\\alpha T)\\), giving \\(\\hat{A}_{t}=\\delta_{t}+\\lambda_{T}(1-d_{t})(\\hat{A}_{t+1}+B_{t})\\). The full Monte Carlo return receives weight \\(\\lambda_{T}^{T-1}\\geq 1-1/\\alpha\\) at the earliest decision and greater weight at later decisions. The bridges correct baseline-coordinate changes but do not alter the Monte Carlo critic target. Stitching remains necessary: \\(G_{t}\\) must include rewards from all later MIRA iterations, even when those iterations occupy different contexts, samples, or learner batches.\nThe governing invariant is that only true environment termination may stop value bootstrapping; infrastructure boundaries never do.\nAppendix J Additional MIRA-AC Results\nAppendix K Behavioral Analysis of MIRA-AC\nThis appendix examines the changes we observe in the model’s meta-reasoning and scientific behavior as a result of training. We compare the cross-environment MIRA-AC policy with untrained MIRA on 400 matched evaluation pairs (800 trajectories): 100 pairs each from symbolic regression, Physics Discovery, HillClimbBench-CPU, and BNLearn. Each pair contains the two policies’ trajectories on the same evaluation instance. We call the held-out evaluation result the gold outcome. In both systems, an outer policy selects the next research direction, an inner executor carries it out, and the harness manages execution and can force continuation after an incomplete iteration.\nK.1 Analysis Protocol\nWe use GPT-5.5 as an LLM judge in two passes. In the first pass, the judge sees the sequence of work orders, executor reports, and proxy feedback under anonymous labels A and B, but not the policy identities or gold outcomes. It records what each trajectory’s policy and executor did, how they responded to evidence, and whether the policy’s next action could resolve an uncertainty visible at the time. This assessment is fixed before the second pass.\nIn the second pass, the judge receives this fixed assessment and each side’s gold outcome; policy identity remains hidden. It separately assesses whether a decision was reasonable when made and whether it ultimately helped or harmed the gold result. The judge also attributes each claimed contribution among the outer policy, inner executor, harness, and unobserved factors. The pair-level verdict used below asks which side’s outer-policy decisions contributed more to its own final outcome. The verdict excludes executor-only and harness-only contributions. Policy identity is revealed only after both passes are complete. All 400 pairs produced valid judgments in both passes.\nFor behavior rates, the unit is a trajectory, not an action. A trajectory is counted once for a behavior if the second-pass judge identifies at least one instance attributable at least partly to the outer policy; repeated instances do not increase the count. Behaviors attributed only to the executor, harness, or unobserved factors are excluded. The exception is productive risk, a whole-trajectory label indicating that a reasonable exploratory bet ultimately improved the gold result. Every rate therefore has a denominator of 400 trajectories per policy.\nThe matched comparisons describe observed differences between the policies. They do not estimate the effect of independently inducing a single behavior: executor stochasticity, interactions among decisions, and the evaluation environment can also affect the final result. The aggregate rates establish how common each behavior was; the excerpts below show what those behaviors looked like in individual trajectories.\nK.2 Pooled Behavioral Changes\nThe outcome-aware judge credited MIRA-AC’s outer-policy decisions with the larger contribution in 203 pairs and untrained MIRA’s in 131; 66 pairs were tied or unclear. MIRA-AC therefore received 60.8% of the 334 decisive attributions. It was also more often classified as taking a defensible risk that improved the gold result (210/400 trajectories, 52.5%, versus 139/400, 34.8%).\nThe remaining rows of Fig. [9](#S6.F9) describe what changed\nin the research process. Relative to untrained MIRA, MIRA-AC more often\nchanged its explanation of the underlying mechanism or the form of its solution\n(185/400 vs. 148/400),\nran a test that distinguished competing ideas (112/400 vs. 67/400), checked\nwhether an improvement held across conditions or edge cases (28/400 vs. 10/400), and made an evidence-supported decision about whether to continue\n(53/400 vs. 29/400). It less often repeated minor variants that yielded\nlittle new information (75/400 vs. 134/400).\nK.3 Selecting and Attributing Trajectory Examples\nFor each environment, the examples include at least one case in which MIRA-AC produced the better gold result and one in which it performed worse than untrained MIRA. We select cases only after computing the aggregate statistics across all trajectories, favoring cases with a clear chain from the available evidence to an outer decision, an executor result, and the gold outcome. Additional cases are included only when they clarify a distinct behavior or the boundary between policy and executor credit. The examples are thus illustrative rather than estimates of how common each behavior was, and every quotation is linked to its original decision or result record.\nA case in which training hurt performance is included only when it reveals an interpretable decision tendency consistent with the training feedback or data distribution, not merely because MIRA-AC lost the pair. Its discussion reconstructs why the choice was reasonable given the information available at the time, explains why the proxy evidence favored a decision that later performed poorly on the gold evaluation, and states any suggested remedy as a testable hypothesis rather than an established correction.\nA useful idea reported by the executor can still have been elicited by the outer loop. We distinguish five relationships between the outer decision and the eventual idea:\n- 1. \nDirect outer specification. The outer work order names the useful hypothesis, test, or planned response before execution.\n- 2. \nOuter-seeded executor discovery. The outer policy isolates the relevant uncertainty or asks for a test likely to resolve it. The executor then supplies the concrete mechanism or implementation.\n- 3. \nState scaffolding. Earlier outer decisions create or preserve a useful best candidate or body of evidence, but do not point the executor toward the eventual idea.\n- 4. \nExecutor-independent pivot. The executor introduces a useful direction outside the requested experiment and its stated contingencies.\n- 5. \nHarness-enabled opportunity. Forced continuation or retry makes later work possible but contributes no research hypothesis. Any later outer decision or executor discovery is credited separately.\nThese categories describe an idea’s provenance, not whether it was good. We require a specific link in content between a work order and a later discovery. An executor idea is not outer-seeded merely because the work order came first, requested a progress update, or was followed by an iteration forced by the harness.\nK.4 Symbolic Regression\nIn symbolic regression, productive research means identifying which variables and mathematical form explain the data, testing that explanation where it is most likely to fail, and resisting improvements that depend on a feature specific to the proxy validation set. Across the 100 matched tasks, MIRA-AC obtained the higher gold score in 58 cases, compared with 32 for untrained MIRA (10 ties). The LLM trajectory judge more often found that MIRA-AC ran a test designed to distinguish two explanations (14% vs. 6%), checked robustness or an edge case (12% vs. 4%), and made a well-supported decision to stop or continue (25% vs. 11%). It also found fewer premature stops (13% vs. 29%) and fewer stretches of repeated minor variants that added little information (3% vs. 13%). These rates count whether a behavior appeared at least once in a trajectory; the matched cases below illustrate how the same tendency to act on specific evidence could help or hurt.\nTesting a specific error reveals a better equation.\nIn MatSci25 (pair symbolic:047), the task asks for a compact equation for stress as a function of strain \\(\\epsilon\\) and temperature \\(T\\). The proxy fit suggested that the coefficient relating stress to strain varied with temperature, but its worst relative errors remained too large. MIRA-AC made that error the target of the next experiment:\nOuter policy (B-D002): “Scan alternative parametric forms for the temperature-dependent modulus \\(E(T)\\) in Stress \\(\\approx\\epsilon E(T)\\) to reduce max relative error and potentially achieve acc_0_1.”\nExecutor (B-R002): “epsilon and T are perfectly correlated (\\(r\\approx 1.0\\)) in the data, so fitting \\(\\epsilon^{p}E(T)\\) with \\(p\\neq 1\\) reduces MRE substantially.”\nThe resulting expression reduced the maximum relative error from 0.140 to 0.052. MIRA-AC scored 0.9949 on the gold evaluation; untrained MIRA scored 0. This was an outer-seeded executor discovery: the outer policy selected the relationship to inspect and the error criterion, which exposed the \\(\\epsilon\\)–\\(T\\) correlation, while the executor introduced the decisive generalization from \\(\\epsilon\\) to \\(\\epsilon^{p}\\). The result therefore supports credit for the policy’s choice of question, not sole credit for the formula.\nOptimizing proxy error preserves a model that does not extrapolate.\nIn the CRK25 reaction-kinetics task (pair symbolic:020), the target depends on time \\(t\\) and concentration \\(A\\). Untrained MIRA tested whether time added information after conditioning on concentration; its executor reported “Pearson(residual,t)=0.0145, confirming t carries no independent predictive power once A is conditioned on” (A-R001), then refined equations using concentration alone until it passed the proxy relative-error threshold. MIRA-AC instead found a more elaborate candidate with the best proxy score, but that expression used a boundary value estimated from the observed data. Its own work order identified the danger:\nOuter policy (B-D005): “The incumbent F34 expression […] achieves acc_0_1=True with max_re=0.052 and NMSE=0.000689, but has 5+ free parameters plus a hardcoded A_max from data maximum — raising OOD generalization risk.”\nExecutor (B-R005): “Discovered Family 38 (F38) — combining rational correction with A-correction in exponent — achieving a 1133\\(\\times\\) NMSE improvement.”\nThe proxy gain was substantial: validation NMSE fell from \\(6.89\\times 10^{-4}\\) to \\(6.08\\times 10^{-7}\\) while satisfying the relative-error criterion. Retaining the model was therefore reasonable given the available metrics, despite the known extrapolation risk. The gold result reversed the proxy ordering: untrained MIRA scored 0.9998 and MIRA-AC scored 0. The executor fitted and compared the candidate equations, but the outer policy directly set the criterion for choosing the final equation and selected the formula after naming its weakness. Forced continuation preserved later opportunities to work, but supplied neither the candidate equation nor the selection criterion.\nInterpretation. Training did not simply make the model change the mathematical form of its answer more often: the judge attributed such a change to the outer policy, or jointly to the outer policy and executor, in 27% of MIRA-AC trajectories and 36% of untrained MIRA trajectories, but those trajectories produced the higher gold score 66.7% and 47.2% of the time, respectively. The two cases show when this tendency helps and when it hurts. A test tied to a systematic pattern in the errors can reveal a better equation; the same pressure to improve a proxy error can favor an expression that uses a constant fitted at the edge of the observed data and may not extrapolate. One testable remedy is to evaluate expressions containing such constants on held-out examples near and beyond the range seen during fitting.\nForced continuation appeared in 57 MIRA-AC trajectories and 17 untrained MIRA trajectories in this environment. The attribution analysis excludes harness-only contributions, but forced continuation gave the policies unequal opportunities to make later decisions. This weakens a causal interpretation of the aggregate difference.\nK.5 Physics Discovery\nPhysics Discovery gives the agent a limited budget for simulator experiments and then evaluates its submitted law on hidden trajectories. Productive research therefore uses an experiment to distinguish physical explanations or test conditions in which they disagree; repeatedly fitting the same acquired trajectories need not produce new evidence. Across 100 matched tasks, MIRA-AC obtained the higher gold score in 65 cases and untrained MIRA in 26 (nine ties). The LLM judge more often observed MIRA-AC revising its mechanism (51% vs. 22%), running a discriminating experiment (28% vs. 15%), and testing an edge case (13% vs. 4%). Repeated minor variations of an unproductive idea fell from 51% to 23%, and premature stopping from 27% to 6%. Perfect gold scores rose only from eight to ten; much of the outcome gain was better partial recovery of the hidden law.\nVarying one parameter separates two candidate laws.\nIn pair physics:012, both trajectories inferred a plausible distance-dependent interaction, but the dependence on the second particle’s parameter remained unresolved. Untrained MIRA stopped after five research iterations even though its executor reported that “14 experiment rounds and unlimited MSE evaluations remain for further improvement” (A-R002). MIRA-AC eventually isolated the ambiguity:\nOuter policy (B-D013): “The cheapest discriminating action is one experiment round varying p2 independently.”\nExecutor (B-R014): “Discovered the correct charge model: \\(p_{1}/p_{2}\\) (not \\(p_{1}/\\sqrt{p_{2}}\\) as previously believed).”\nMIRA-AC then preserved its best prior candidate when later changes to the numerical integration failed to improve the fit. It received a gold score of 1.000, compared with 0.005 for untrained MIRA. The outer policy selected the controlled parameter variation and, in the following work order, made the competing coupling laws explicit. The executor ran the experiment and identified the exact relation. This is not an independent executor pivot. A forced continuation preserved the opportunity to run the following iteration, but the preceding policy decision had already selected the informative parameter variation.\nAfter finding the law, further exploration chases a proxy metric.\nPair physics:001 shows the same willingness to act on uncertainty after the model had largely resolved which physical law applied. MIRA-AC’s executor first reported, “Discovered the force law: F = -p1*p2 * r_hat / r2” (A-R002). The outer policy nevertheless redirected the remaining experiment budget:\nOuter policy (A-D007): “pivot directly to acquiring diverse trajectories to improve trajectory_shaping_quality.”\nExecutor (A-R007): “0.006 gain over 6 rounds.”\nUntrained MIRA instead instructed its executor to “probe distance scaling, sign reversal, and magnitude coupling” (B-D002), validated the inverse-square law, and scored 1.000 on the gold evaluation. MIRA-AC exhausted all 15 experiment rounds and scored 0.00085. Acquiring broader trajectories could reasonably have exposed a hidden failure and did improve the visible trajectory_shaping_quality score, but the new trajectories no longer distinguished between competing physical laws. The costly pivot was directly specified by the outer policy; the executor implemented it. Forced continuation made later work possible but supplied no scientific direction.\nInterpretation. The principal change in Physics Discovery was not simply that MIRA-AC did more work. It was more willing to run a new experiment when uncertainty remained. That tendency helped when the uncertainty distinguished physical laws and hurt when the policy continued optimizing a proxy metric after competing physical laws were no longer in question. A policy trained to seek incremental improvements in proxy score could learn this preference, although the matched trajectories alone cannot establish that explanation. A direct follow-up is to require each costly experiment to state which competing physical laws it is meant to distinguish, or stop collecting data solely to improve the proxy metric after several rounds with negligible gains.\nK.6 HillClimbBench-CPU: Larger Architectural Changes\nHillClimbBench-CPU asks the agent to improve a neural architecture under a fixed CPU training budget. Productive research must therefore do more than change hyperparameters: it must identify a computational bottleneck, propose an architecture that addresses it, and distinguish a genuine improvement from a proxy-evaluation shortcut. Across 100 matched pairs, MIRA-AC achieved the higher gold score in 55, compared with 45 for untrained MIRA. Its trajectories were more often judged to change the model’s computation rather than only its settings (73% vs. 60%), or to take a productive risk (66% vs. 55%), and less often remained in an unproductive sequence of minor variants after they stopped improving (23% vs. 41%). The shift was not uniformly beneficial: continuing despite contrary evidence was also more common (26% vs. 10%). When the judge examined the research process without seeing the final scores, it preferred untrained MIRA in 53 pairs and MIRA-AC in 45 (two ties). MIRA-AC’s gold-score advantage therefore came from decisions with larger effects on the final outcomes, not from a uniformly better research process.\nAn architecture change that matched the task.\nIn the scaled-Cayley forgetting task, the model had to retain a Cayley-rotation structure while extrapolating to longer horizons and larger amplitudes. After the current architecture reached an extrapolation ceiling, MIRA-AC’s outer policy identified the restrictive component: “Allowing scale > 1 enables growth modes while preserving the Cayley rotation structure” (B-D006). The executor implemented a clamped exponential scale and reported, “Score: 0.5710 (delta +0.4008 vs incumbent 0.1703),” with extrapolation rising from 0.072 to 0.615 (B-R006). The final gold score was 0.683, compared with 0.195 for the matched untrained trajectory, which continued to explore nearby direct-mapping variants.\nThe ability to model growth was specified directly by the outer policy. A later executor report introduced a diagonal preconditioner, which scales different internal modes by different amounts, but only after the outer policy had focused the search on scaling those modes; we therefore treat that detail as an outer-seeded executor discovery rather than an independent pivot. The executor supplied part of the implementation, while the outer loop had already identified the model’s inability to scale different internal modes as the bottleneck.\nThe same willingness to change the architecture can follow the wrong signal.\nThe Universal Transformer task evaluates a model that repeatedly applies the same block and learns when to stop while solving cellular automata. MIRA-AC instead ordered the executor to “Switch to a direct local-neighbor transition” (B-D001). This was plausible given the available evidence: cellular-automaton updates are local, and successive refinements raised the proxy score to 0.985. The gold evaluator nevertheless assigned the final candidate a score of 0 after marking it invalid. Untrained MIRA preserved the benchmark’s intended design—“Single weight-tied attention+MLP block reused across steps” (A-D001)—and scored 0.809 on the gold evaluation.\nBoth architectures were specified directly by their respective outer policies; the contrast is not explained by an independent executor discovery or a harness-provided hypothesis. The trained policy made a meaningful architectural bet, but the proxy signal rewarded a solution tailored to the proxy evaluator that did not survive the gold evaluation. The zero alone does not show whether the candidate violated the intended architecture or merely failed because of an implementation error. Because the actor was trained to seek incremental improvements in proxy score, selecting a rapidly improving proxy candidate was aligned with its training signal even though that candidate failed gold evaluation. One useful safeguard would require architecture-changing work orders to state which intended architectural constraint they preserve and to pass basic validity tests across input shapes before being selected as the best candidate. Whether this retains the gains from architecture search while filtering proxy shortcuts remains to be tested.\nTogether, the two cases clarify the aggregate shift. MIRA-AC more often pivoted from repeated minor variants to changes in the computation itself. That behavior created value when the proposed architecture addressed the true bottleneck, but produced a larger downside when the proxy evaluator rewarded an easier shortcut.\nK.7 BNLearn: Resolving Remaining Graph Uncertainty\nBNLearn asks the agent to recover a causal directed acyclic graph from observational data and a limited intervention budget. An intervention fixes one variable to a chosen value and reveals how other variables respond. Useful exploration is usually conditional: choose an intervention that separates a few remaining graph hypotheses, or search a small, explicitly enumerated set of edge edits after localizing the remaining errors. We summarize those errors using structural Hamming distance (SHD), the number of missing, extra, or reversed edges. Because many trajectories reach the score ceiling, different research paths often have the same final score. The outcome-aware judge credited MIRA-AC with decisions that contributed more to its own final outcome in 25 of 100 pairs and untrained MIRA in 22, with 53 ties. MIRA-AC trajectories more often contained a test aimed at resolving uncertainty about a specific edge (55% vs. 34%) and a productive risk (61% vs. 40%), but less often preserved the strongest current graph while deciding what to try next (19% vs. 32%). BNLearn was held out from actor training, although it was included in critic pretraining; these differences therefore reflect behavior transferred from other training environments.\nTargeted interventions fix the remaining graph errors.\nOn bnlearn:004, MIRA-AC reduced its graph to one missing, one extra, and one reversed edge. Its outer policy then selected two interventions: “Intervene on X0” and “Intervene on X16” (A-D003). The executor reported, “Fixed all 3 SHD errors using 2 strategic interventions (X0, X16)” (A-R003), identifying the precise reversal, deletion, and addition implied by the new evidence about which variables were affected. MIRA-AC achieved a gold score of 1.0; untrained MIRA repeatedly launched related combinatorial searches after visible wall-time failures and finished at 0.833.\nThe outer policy receives direct credit for selecting the two informative interventions and the narrowly defined correction problem. The executor receives credit for identifying the exact three edge edits; because those edits followed from the requested interventions, this is an outer-seeded executor discovery rather than an executor-independent pivot. The useful behavior was not exploration in the abstract, but spending scarce interventions on observations that could change a small set of decisions.\nBroad search loses to a one-edge repair.\nThe reverse pattern appears on bnlearn:057. Untrained MIRA used two interventions to raise its score for recovering which variables affect one another from 0.935 to 0.982, then issued the narrow work order “close the SHD=1 gap by identifying and adding the single missing edge” (B-D009). The executor compared the effects implied by the current graph with the variables affected by each intervention and reported, “Identified and added the single missing edge X15-¿X14 to close the SHD=1 gap” (B-R009). Untrained MIRA therefore reached a gold score of 1.0. MIRA-AC’s reconstruction based on its interventions made substantial progress, but its early searches incurred wall-time failures and later graph-learning methods and exhaustive edge-swap searches concluded that the current SHD=7 graph was “the best achievable” (A-R011). It finished at 0.844.\nUntrained MIRA’s outer policy directly asked the executor to find the one missing edge; the executor supplied the exact edge, making the solution an outer-seeded executor discovery. MIRA-AC’s broader search was reasonable after several methods agreed on the same best graph, but it failed to turn the available evidence into a sufficiently narrow correction. This is consistent with a preference learned in other environments for gathering new evidence, even in an environment where many near-ceiling solutions require only a small repair to the remaining graph errors. A late-stage work order could instead test the smallest set of edge edits consistent with the intervention evidence before rebuilding the graph or expanding to a broad search.\nBNLearn therefore shows both sides of the same tendency. Targeted experiments can close the last graph errors efficiently, but exploration that begins before the remaining errors are identified can spend the remaining budget without improving the graph. The large number of tied outcomes also means that many differences in behavior do not appear in final-score comparisons.\nK.8 Cross-Environment Interpretation\nAcross the four environments, training did not simply increase the amount of exploration. The trained outer policy more often acted on unresolved uncertainty: run a test that could separate competing explanations, change the form of the solution when the current class of solutions stopped improving, or abandon a line of minor variants that had stopped yielding new information. In Physics Discovery and HillClimbBench-CPU this often produced larger changes to the proposed physical law or neural architecture. In symbolic regression and BNLearn it more often appeared as a controlled test or a focused attempt to resolve the remaining errors.\nThe same shift explains the observed degradations. Acting on uncertainty was valuable when resolving that uncertainty improved the gold evaluation, but costly when a proxy metric rewarded a shortcut, a constant fitted to the edge of the observed data, or additional data collection after the central question was already resolved. The trained policy therefore made the model’s research decisions more likely to change the final result, for better or worse, rather than uniformly more rigorous. The paired examples suggest three concrete directions for future evaluation: require costly experiments to name the hypotheses they separate, test architecture and expression changes on deliberately shifted or out-of-distribution cases before selecting them as the final answer, and prefer the smallest set of possible edits once only a few uncertainties remain.", "segments": null}, {"item_id":
